      **Definition 11.11 (state seal).** A state seal at journal position `n` is a compressed snapshot of the whole state of the space after the first `n` entries: the interner, the identity catalog and the telemetry windows of the engine, and for each graph its hot elements, output layers and the state of every operator of its plan, and of the host the served patch history, the event ring (both capped, D30), the catalog history, the definition texts, the adapter state and, in a live run, the positions and the last 20,000 delivery keys. It carries a description: `n`, the identity of the engine build (the module version id of the assembly), the kernel digest, the state digest, the catalog digest, the generation, the definition texts and the heads. It is stored next to the journal (a `seal` table in SQLite, chunk items `s:{n20}:{i}` and then the item that names them in Cosmos, so an incomplete seal is no seal), the latest two are kept, and the host takes one every `--seal-every` entries (default 5000) while it holds its lock. It is not the snapshot seal of Def. 11.8, which is a set of pages of one graph at one revision in object storage and is not built.
      **Proposition 11.10 (restart from a state seal).** Started on a store that has a state seal and a journal, a space builds a host from the definition texts of the latest seal, accepts the seal only if the build identity and the kernel digest equal those of the running engine, the catalog digest of those definitions equals the sealed one, and the state digest of the loaded state equals the sealed one, replays the entries after `n` comparing each recomputed commit with the stored one, and serves the same sequence numbers, heads, state digest and `/space` as a restart that replays the whole journal. A seal that fails any check is not used: a message says why and the whole journal is replayed. Status: **proved** from Proposition 11.9 under one more premise; read only, checked by no tool. *Premise.* (SL1; called S1 in earlier revisions, renamed because S1 is also a store property of Def. 11.9 and a space judgment of Def. 5.8) A state loaded from a seal at position `n` equals the state of the engine after the first `n` journal entries in every part that a later entry reads or that the space serves. *Proof.* By Proposition 11.9 the state after `n` entries is a function of those entries. By SL1 the loaded state is that state, as far as the entries after `n` can observe. Processing the tail from equal states with the same inputs gives equal results (Theorem 6.3), so the space after the tail equals the space of a restart that replays the whole journal. ∎ The engine checks SL1 only through the state digest, which covers the graph states and the telemetry and not every field (the interner, the identity catalog, support counts). SL1 is **tested by examples** and wanted as a generated test (test T-SEAL, [inventory.md](inventory.md)). Tests: a restart from a seal replays only the tail and gives the same space, on memory and SQLite (and Cosmos when an emulator is given, which this paper did not run); the space then continues after a seal restore exactly as after a full replay; a seal that does not check out is refused and the journal replays; a definition commit before the seal survives; the commits after the seal are verified against the store; and a snapshot loaded into a fresh engine saves to the same bytes and the engine continues like the original, at several points of the fixtures. **What it does not check:** the commits at or before `n` are not recomputed on a restart from a seal, so their agreement with the stored commit log rests on the state digest of the seal and not on a comparison, and a seal written by one build is refused by every other build, so the first restart after a deploy replays the whole journal. This is the documented trust model of a seal, and it was shown end to end (`paper/evidence/restart-seal-prefix-head6.txt`). On a fixture journal of 17 entries with a seal at entry 10, deleting entry 5 (an input batch before the seal) leaves a restart from the seal that succeeds and serves a `/space` byte-equal to the first start; its start message says how many revisions came from the seal and that the 10 entries before it were not read. `serve --verify-journal` ignores seals: it replays the whole journal, compares every recomputed commit with the stored log, checks the state at the seal's position against the seal's digest, and refuses this journal at revision 3 of `master`. `RestartSmokeTests` has the same case. An earlier engine printed that every revision had been reproduced even when a seal had supplied most of them (`restart-seal-prefix-head4.txt`). The check of the sealed prefix is therefore the seal's own digests, a full verification of a journal is a restart with `--verify-journal`, and the cost of that is the cost of a restart without a seal. Tested refusals are a wrong sealed digest, another build and truncated bytes; damage that leaves the state digest equal (in the event ring or the state inside an operator, if the digest does not cover them) was not tested.
    - **Worked example.** [R02] section 4.8 traces revision 42, a retype of `net.ObservedFlow` to `net.ObservedHttpCall`, through the commit record, the relationship partition (history row and new `cur` in one batch), both endpoint partitions (adjacency and uniqueness items) and the telemetry rows, which do not create revision 42 or any other.

    ## 11.8 Telemetry plane storage

    | Tier | Horizon | Store | Basis |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | Hot | last 60 minutes at source resolution | in-process ring per `(element, property)`, compressed | parameter. Compression: delta-of-delta timestamps and XOR values averaged 1.37 bytes per point on Facebook monitoring data (lit, [pelkonen2015]) |
    | Warm | 30 days at 60 s, hot cache 7 days | ADX table `ElementTelemetry` | parameter. Continuous ingestion from Event Hubs and `make-series` analytics (doc, [adx-eventhub], [adx-timeseries]) |
    | Cold | 400 days at 5 min and 1 h | ADX materialized views over the warm table | parameter. Views aggregate incrementally and a query combines the materialized part with the unprocessed delta (doc, [adx-matviews]) |
    | External | as the source keeps it | query-through to Azure Monitor managed Prometheus (18 months) and platform metrics (93 days, 1-minute collection) | doc ([azmon-prom], [azmon-metrics]). Copying doubles cost |

    - **Retention and downsampling.** Per table `SoftDeletePeriod`, with cache policy independent of retention (doc, [adx-retention], [adx-cache]). Roll-up follows the kind (Def. 7.8, Theorem 7.2): `windowSum` sums, `distribution` merges buckets, `gauge` keeps last and extrema, `counter` converts to increments first.
    - **Latency.** Queued ADX ingestion batches up to 5 minutes by default. Streaming ingestion targets sub-second latency and 4 MB per request (doc, [adx-ingestion], [adx-streaming]). Projections never wait on ADX. They consume closed-window records from the engine's own window operator (Def. 7.7). ADX serves history. Database cursors give exactly-once reads for offline re-derivation, with up to 60 s lag under streaming ingestion (doc, [adx-cursors]).
    - **Query-through limits** (doc). A PromQL query spans at most 32 days and returns at most 500,000 series per metric. A workspace accepts 1 M active series and 1 M events per minute by default ([azmon-limits], [azmon-ingest-limits]). Query-through is for bounded scopes and presentation. It never feeds a structure-emitting projection (Theorem 7.3).
    - **Not in Cosmos.** Series count ≈ 2 M (240,000 flow relationships × 3, 150,000 pods × 5, 300,000 resources × 2; assumption). One write per series per minute is ≈ 35,000 writes/s, ≈ 200,000 RU/s at the 6 RU assumption. Cosmos stores no telemetry, not even a latest value.
    - **Hot ring size.** 2 M series × 60 points × 1.37 bytes ≈ 165 MB per hour of horizon, if the cited ratio carries over (assumption; E16).
    - **Lookback state of `Series`.** A `Series` with lookback `within` over series of period `P` keeps `within / P` closed windows per series in operator state ([R03] section 11.2: memory bounded by `within` times keys). For ≈ 2 M series, `P = 60 s` and `within = 24 h` (the lookback of the example in [R03] section 8.3) that is ≈ 2.9 × 10⁹ windows (arithmetic on assumption), far beyond the hot-graph budget of H9 and beyond the 60-minute hot horizon above. Lookbacks must be sized to the largest real consumer and long horizons rolled up to coarser windows first (Theorem 7.2). Closed windows reach the operator as recorded inputs (Def. 7.7), so evaluation never queries ADX.
    - **Element view across tiers.** For `[τ₁, τ₂)` inside the hot horizon the ring is read for the element set at `r`. Otherwise ADX is queried with the key list in chunks of at most 10,000 keys (parameter). Whole-graph joins need a verified way to read sealed pages from ADX (risk R17). A time-aligned view ("structure valid at each `τ`") maps `τ` to the last revision whose logical time is at most `τ` via commit records, at the cost of one structural read per distinct revision.

    ## 11.9 Hot graph layout (engine-internal, behind the port)

    All items are design parameters to be tested by the benchmarks of Ch. 13.
    - **Interning.** Every entity, relationship, type and property id maps to a dense `int` through an append-only intern table. Hot structures use indexes. Strings appear only at the boundary. Seals use the same mapping as dictionary-encoded columns (doc, [arrow-columnar]).
    - **Struct of arrays.** Entities as parallel columns plus one typed column per ontology property, sparse by type. Columnar vector-at-a-time evaluation avoided tuple-at-a-time interpretation overhead and gave one to two orders of magnitude over earlier engines on TPC-H (lit, [boncz2005]).
    - **Adjacency.** A base CSR sorted by `(vertex, traversal direction, type)` plus a per-vertex append-only delta log whose entries carry creation and invalidation revisions. Per-vertex edge logs with creation and invalidation timestamps keep scans sequential under concurrent transactions (lit, [zhu2020]). Deltas merge into a new CSR in the background. Teseo is the alternative if merge pauses hurt (lit, [deleo2021]).
    - **MVCC and snapshots.** Base arrays are immutable once published. A reader pins a revision and filters deltas by `from ≤ r < to`. Pinning copies nothing. Seals write Arrow IPC pages. The .NET implementation documents the IPC formats, no memory-mapped read and no compute kernels (doc, [arrow-dotnet], [arrow-status]).
    - **Large arrays.** Objects of 85,000 bytes or more go to the large object heap, collected only in generation 2 and not compacted by default (doc, [dotnet-loh]). Columns live in fixed segments of 64 K elements, recycled through `ArrayPool<T>` (doc, [dotnet-arraypool]), copy-on-write per segment.
    - **Vectorized operators.** Linear operators run on batches of about 1,000 rows over `Span<T>` (doc, [dotnet-spans]) with `Vector128<T>` kernels (doc, [dotnet-simd]). `TensorPrimitives` is an option whose package was a release candidate at the time of reading (doc, [dotnet-tensor]).
    - **Read-mostly lookups** use `FrozenDictionary` (doc, [dotnet-frozen]).
    - **Backpressure.** Bounded channels between adapter, acceptance, evaluator, committer and follower, with `Wait` (doc, [dotnet-channels]). Structural inputs are never dropped. The telemetry ring may drop the oldest under overload, with a loss counter that marks affected windows `Partial` (Def. 7.7).

    ## 11.10 Risks and the experiments that settle them

    | Id | Risk | Experiment |
    |---|---|---|
    | R1 | one log segment is one logical partition limited to 10,000 RU/s. Without group commit ≈ 250 commits/s at ≈ 40 RU per commit-only write (assumption) | **E1** synthetic patch stream into a real account: group sizes 1, 8, 64, patch sizes 1, 4, 32 edits. If below 2,000 revisions/s at the best setting, split masters per cluster |
    | R2 | write RU per item is undocumented. Commit-only ≈ 40 RU and commit plus materialization ≈ 100 RU per 4-edit revision, from ≈ 6 RU per 1 KB write (assumption) | **E2** record the request charge per batch shape |
    | R3 | materialization lag makes overlay reads expensive | **E3** commit-to-`wm` latency, steady and peak |
    | R4 | hubs exceed a logical partition | **E4** replay a day of aggregated flows, degree distribution, bucket splitting |
    | R5 | flow rate and churn assumptions | **E5** on the lab AKS cluster with Cilium |
    | R6 | nondeterministic store reads break replay | **E6** same log into in-memory, SQLite, Cosmos, compare seal digests |
    | R7 | emulator gaps | **E7** conformance suite on the emulator in CI |
    | R8 | crash between protocol steps, at-least-once feed | **E8** fault injection after each step |
    | R9 | history and log volume ≈ 60 GB/day (assumption: 50 revisions/s × 4 edits × ≈ 1.2 KB per element state, with three states stored per edit: one in the history and before and after in the log; the factor of three is this paper's reading of [R02]) | **E9** bytes per revision |
    | R10 | canonicalization change | **E10** (Theorem 1.6) |
    | R11 | PostgreSQL may be simpler at MVP scale | **E11** same port on Azure PostgreSQL, run E1 and E6 |
    | R12 | two writers | **E12** expect 409 for the loser, no fork (Theorem 11.2) |
    | R13 | aligned frontiers | out of storage scope. Commit records keep frontiers so an aligned mode can be built on `Follow` |
    | R14 | multi-hop patterns are not constant-time (Fact 11.2) | **E14** classify the first ten real projections, measure cost against hub degree |
    | R15 | hot graph outgrows one node | **E15** B9 at 1×, 4×, 16× |
    | R16 | telemetry-derived structure flaps or breaks replay | **E16** (O7.3) |
    | R17 | structure-telemetry joins from sealed snapshots in ADX | **E17** spike |
    | R18 | Arrow .NET gaps | **E18** B10 with Arrow IPC against a plain columnar format, keep Arrow if within 20% |
    | R19 | GC pauses at hot-graph size | **E19** B4 and B6 with GC tracing |
    | R20 | ≈ 2 M series against the 1 M active-series default of the Prometheus workspace | **E20** count series from lab data |

    ## 11.11 Delta to iteration 0.1

    - **Gap 6.** A storage port with write, commit, index, snapshot and follow operations (Def. 11.3) replaces the missing write side. `IIdentityCatalog` writes ride in the commit batch (finding 7).
    - **Gap 7 and finding 2.** Integer revisions. 64-bit values as strings (finding 24). Pattern push-down is not a port feature: traversal stays in the engine.
    - **Finding 25.** The seal carries the frontier.
    - **Telemetry.** A second port without revisions (Def. 11.4). `TelemetryObservation` gains time (Ch. 7).
    - **Uniqueness.** Enforced by the single writer against the hot graph (Theorem 11.8). The relationship uniqueness index key becomes `(tenant, graph, source, target, type)` (finding 1).
    - **`GraphExtent`** gains version intervals on the engine-side port so overlay reads can be checked.
    - **Request and response streams** with `RequestId` stay at the remote boundary. Inside the process the storage port uses ordinary async calls with explicit batching.

    ## 11.12 Deviations found by the prototype

    Ids refer to Ch. 14 (14.8).

    - **D8.** The telemetry port has two operations that Def. 11.4 lacks: `MarkPartial`, and a `Deleted` flag on a sample, which retracts a window (needed for `revise`, Proposition 6.8).
    - **D35.** The telemetry store's retention (48 h behind the newest window seen) is not the declared, frontier-based retention of Def. 7.13 (Ch. 7).
    - **D10.** The storage port is synchronous and `Follow` returns a list, not a stream. Identity bindings and the frontier travel in the commit batch as specified.
    - **Adapters.** In-memory, SQLite and Cosmos behind one conformance suite. The SQLite commit is one transaction with the watermark equal to the head.
    - **Cosmos store (D23)** (`sattle replay CAPTURE --store cosmos=ENDPOINT[,db=NAME][,drop]`). What it is:
      - *Containers.* `log`, `state`, `identity` and `leases`, with hierarchical partition keys led by tenant and graph (`/t`, `/g`, then `/s`, `/k`, `/d` and `/n`). Section 11.5 gave `identity` and `leases` other keys and had a `lens` container, which the engine does not create because nothing uses it.
      - *Commit point.* The creation of the commit records in one transactional batch per segment (the revision shifted right by 16) of at most 100 records and about 1.5 MB. A create that answers 409 means another writer made the revision, and the store answers `Conflict`. A commit whose edits exceed 1,000,000 characters writes them first as chunk items `p:{r20}:{i}` of 400,000 characters and creates the commit record, which names the chunk count, last; chunks without a record are not a commit and a retry overwrites them (step 2 of 11.6).
      - *One writer.* A lease item in `leases` (an owner and an expiry of 30 seconds, renewed at half, released on dispose) refuses a second writer even when it has the right head, where step 1 of 11.6 has an epoch that correctness does not depend on. A reader that does not hold the lease follows the log of the holder.
      - *Materialization.* It runs inside `Commit`, as one idempotent transactional batch per logical partition that the commit touches, 16 batches at a time. The version rows and the adjacency rows of an entity share its partition. The writer keeps a hot copy of the head (its mirror) to validate a patch before writing and to know at which revision each version began, so an old version row is written and `cur` replaced without a read, and a closed adjacency interval is replaced by its derived id without a query. A retry after a crash falls back to an idempotent path per edit.
      - *Identity bindings and watermark.* Bindings are written after the commit record, in one batch per domain partition and commit; a crash in between leaves a committed revision whose bindings a replay recreates (Theorem 1.4), not an orphan binding. The watermark is an upsert in `leases` every 64 commits.
      - *Authentication.* Entra (`DefaultAzureCredential`) for an account. The emulator's published local key is used only when the endpoint is a loopback address, and no key is read from a file.
      - *Differences from the protocol of 11.6* (read from the code, not found by a test): materialization inside the commit call means no watermark lag and no overlay, so H13 does not apply; bindings come after the commit record and not before it; the watermark is an upsert and not an `If-Match` replace in `log/meta`; a group that spans two segments or more than 100 records is several batches, each atomic, and the group is not.
      - *Not built.* A snapshot of one element above 2 MB (an element state is one item), bulk execution in the SDK, anything on a real account.
    - **What is verified for Cosmos, and by whom.** Everything below was run against the vNext emulator by sets B and A. This paper had no emulator and repeated none of it.
      - *Tests.* Set A reports a full engine run with the emulator of 591 of 591 tests at evaluation 7. This paper ran 572 without it (456 default, 96 slow, 20 on the captures; `paper/evidence/tests-head7.txt`), so 19 cases need the emulator; which ones was not itemized. The Cosmos-specific cases of the store tests: a commit of 3.6 MB reads back whole on all three stores, chunks without a commit record are not a commit, and a second writer is refused by the lease until the first lets go or the lease expires.
      - *A real capture.* The newest capture (160,300 records, 2,099 revisions in 9 graphs, about 59,000 elements at head) replayed onto the emulator gives the state digest of the memory store, before the batched writes (evaluation 4) and after them (evaluation 5); it was not repeated at evaluation 7, where the digest has another value.

        | | Before batching (evaluation 4) | After batching (evaluation 5) |
        |---|---|---|
        | Requests, set A | 195,605: 2,099 commit, 52,307 identity, 78,239 materialize, 60,481 read, 2,211 meta, 242 query, 26 follow | 67,181: 2,099 commit, 2,159 identity, 62,745 materialize, 61 read, 91 meta, 26 follow (`paper/evidence/cosmos-cap-orch-turn8.txt`) |
        | Requests, set B | 195,669 | 67,193 |
        | Requests per revision | 93 | 32 |
        | Wall time | 1,607 s (set B; 7.8 s in memory) | 230 s (set A), 276 s (set B) |

        On the fixtures (20 revisions) set B reports 219 requests where there were 547. The 62,745 materialize requests are the floor of this design, one batch per touched element partition, about 1.06 per element at head, because a transactional batch cannot span partitions.
      - *What this does not say.* The emulator reports a request charge of 1 per request, so **request counts are the only cost evidence**: there is no RU figure, and no real account was measured. No commit of this capture exceeded the inline limit, so the chunk path was not taken in it. The capture spans 1,017 s and the replay took 230 to 276 s, so the emulator on this loaded machine kept up with the rate of the capture by a factor of about 4; that is arithmetic on one run each, without group commit, and says nothing about the latency or the RU limits of an account. The latency figures of H5 and H7 on the emulator are in Ch. 13.
    - **Journal and restart (D27).** A served space keeps its journal in the store it commits to and restarts by replaying it (11.7, Def. 11.10, Proposition 11.9). Ch. 11 had a seal plus a log tail for the hot graph (Def. 11.1, 11.8); the engine first had no seal, so every restart replayed the whole journal and verified every commit, at a cost linear in the journal (Ch. 13, H10). It now has a **state seal** (D28, Def. 11.11, Proposition 11.10) that is a snapshot of the whole engine state and not the page set of Def. 11.8: a restart loads it and replays the tail, and the whole journal is replayed when no seal is usable. The journal and the seals live in the same SQLite file or Cosmos database as the commit log, not in object storage.
    - **Bounded served history (D30).** The host serves `GET /graphs/{g}/patches`, snapshots at older revisions and the event stream from memory, and an earlier engine kept every patch of every graph and the last 100,000 events. The host now keeps the last `--retain-patches` patches per graph (default 2,000, 0 keeps all) and the last `--retain-events` events (default 20,000), and drops the oldest in one cut when the retained ones are 10 percent over. Revision numbers, heads, `seq`, the state and every digest are unchanged; a request for a revision older than the first retained one answers `410 Gap` with the first revision still available, and a subscriber that resumes before the oldest retained event gets `gap` and snapshots (tested: heads, state and sequence do not depend on the retention at three settings, revisions inside the retention read as without a cap and older ones answer `Gap`, a resuming subscriber gets exactly what it missed or `gap`). The cap is on what the host serves from memory, not on what the store keeps: the commit log and the journal are complete (no protocol route reads an older revision from the store, so a client that needs one gets `Gap`). It is what lets a state seal stay the same size however long the history is (Ch. 13, H10). The catalog history is not capped.
    - **State seal (D28).** The engine's seal differs from the seal of Def. 11.8 in what it is for and what it trusts. It is a field-by-field snapshot of the live objects of one engine build (about 1.9 million objects at one hour of history, loaded into a host built from the same definitions), so it is valid only for the build that wrote it, and not for another consumer. A restart from it does not recompute the commits at or before its position (Proposition 11.10). It is taken while the host holds its lock, which stalls input for 2 to 3 s per seal in set B's runs (`engine/PERF.md`, "Turn 8"; not re-run by this paper).
    - **Hot graph.** The first hot `Graph` was the reference structure (string ids, a record per element, hash sets for adjacency): 2,395 MB for 750,000 entities and 2.5 M relationships, 6 times the budget of H9. The second layout takes **337 MB** in one run, about 100 bytes per element (`engine/PERF.md`). It differs from 11.9 in two points: adjacency is a linked list per endpoint, not a CSR with a per-vertex delta log, so removal costs `O(degree)`; and the API still takes and returns string ids and records, so an element is materialized from columns on every read, which is why a point read costs 250 to 410 ns instead of 20 ns. `HotGraphTests` compares every operation on random edit sequences against `ReferenceGraph`. MVCC pinning of revisions (11.9) is not part of it: the engine has one writer and the store keeps versions. Whole-engine memory on the 240,000-pair estate is not at the target: 2.5 GB after structure and 5.4 GB at the end (`engine/PERF.md`, Ch. 13, 13.7), because the in-memory store keeps element and adjacency versions and every patch, and the support layers, lineage records and identity catalog are on the object heap.
    - **Telemetry port.** In-memory ring, 7.84 M samples/s for `Append` (`engine/out/bench-final.txt`).

    ## 11.13 Obligations

    | Id | Statement | Discharged by |
    |---|---|---|
    | O11.1 | Theorems 11.1 to 11.5 and 11.8 hold for each adapter (done for in-memory, SQLite and the emulator; E6 done once on a real capture on the emulator, with equal digests) | port conformance suite on a real account; E6 and E12 on a real account, E8 (fault injection is built for the chunk path only) |
    | O11.2 | The adapter returns reads in canonical order with layout-independent continuations (Theorem 11.6 (ii)) | E6 |
    | O11.3 | Every number marked assumption or parameter is replaced by a measurement or revised | E1 to E20, reported in Ch. 14 |
    | O11.4 | The seal digest and page size are fixed in the descriptor | schema change and conformance test |


## Ch. 12 Outputs: documents and canvas

The whole chapter as it stood before the reader rewrite (2026-11-12). The rewrite keeps the definitions, theorems, tests and measurements in plain words; the UI stack, the protocol routes, file names, the mismatch table of the first export, the delta and the obligations are kept only here.

    # Ch. 12 Outputs: documents and canvas

    **Claim.** A graph has no outputs of its own. Everything a person sees is produced by a subscriber of a graph feed, a snapshot followed by ordered patches. A document is a derived graph rendered deterministically, and every response is identified by a digest of its content.

    **Status.**
    - **Built, engine side.** Protocol version 3 (through 3.13, `engine/PROTOCOL.md`): one ordered, resumable event stream; resources revalidated by content tags; definitions served as the graph `catalog`; drafts, releases, bounded preview with identity effects, refactoring; edits to data graphs; lineage in both directions; references for missing endpoints, also alone (a paged route, and a file per graph in the static output); quantities and units in the palette; meters with thresholds, also as a file per graph in the static output; the `Lift` operator in the palette, the skeleton and the draft edits (3.13).
    - **Built, canvas** (`canvas/`: TypeScript, React 19, Fluent UI v9, one store, a WebGL2 graph view). Views of the data graph, of the graph space on one time axis, of the ontology and of documents; a projection editor in which every operator kind can be built, `Lift` included (12.4); color by value role. For levels of abstraction (Ch. 5, 5.8): a default source that is the engine's own output on the synthetic estate, a highlight that follows lineage up and down, stubs for references, and supporting elements collapsed onto what they support (12.4).
    - **Tested, current commit.** From set A (runs of the project that the authors did not repeat; Ch. 14, 14.1) at the canvas of evaluation 11 against the engine of evaluation 10: a clean type check, 672 tests, the accessibility script with all checks passing, and the walkthroughs strict on the `estate` fixture, 10 of 10. This paper ran none of it.
    - **Tested, by this paper** at the canvas of evaluation 8 against the engines of evaluation 7 and evaluation 9 (Ch. 14; the figures are the same on both unless stated): 643 unit and component tests in 33 files, a clean type check, the walkthroughs strict on the `estate` fixture (8 of 8), the accessibility script (0 violations in 32 page and dialog checks, 12 keyboard and highlight checks, role colors in 3 themes), and the default source regenerated byte for byte. Seen in a browser in set A and not checked by a script of this paper: the stubs and the collapse on the served newest capture.
    - **Not built or not verified.** The canvas at the canvas of evaluation 11 reads what protocol 3.12 added (the references route, named stubs and telemetry in the default source, which is regenerated from the engine: 94 files); this paper read the commit and the canvas README and did not check it in a browser. In the default source the document is the head only, and telemetry arrives all at once after the history, not aligned with the patches. Also: the `dotnet run` path of the generator of the default source; browsers other than Chrome; a screen reader pass; an edit UI for data graphs through lens views (the engine endpoint exists, Ch. 8); a replica digest compared with the engine's at each revision (O12.1); document truncation (O12.7). The color override per graph lives in the browser and not on the engine.

    **Gate.** Phase 2: what a person sees (2b) and that it follows the stream (2c). The projection editor is early evidence for gate 1b. Its propositions on replicas, tags and documents belong to gate 1a.
    Uses: Ch. 1 (identity), Ch. 3 (graph, revision, snapshot), Ch. 4 (patches), Ch. 6 (T8 handoff), Ch. 7 (series, element view), Ch. 9 (provenance record), Ch. 11 (follow, seal).
    Used by: Ch. 14.
    Sources: `canvas/README.md`, `canvas/PERF.md`, `engine/README.md`, `engine/PROTOCOL.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md` "Document projections", [R01] T14 and findings, `lab/CAPTURE-FORMAT.md`.

    ## 12.1 Consumers of graph feeds

    A graph has no outputs of its own. Everything a person sees is produced by a **subscriber** of graph feeds: the canvas draws graphs, a document projection renders blocks, an external system mirrors state. All of them use the one mechanism of Ch. 3 and Ch. 11: a sealed snapshot followed by adjacent revisions.

    **Definition 12.1 (graph feed).** The feed of graph `g` is the pair of a snapshot `Snap(r_s)` with its seal and the stream of commit records `r_s + 1, r_s + 2, …` from `Follow` (Def. 11.3), plus, separately, the telemetry feed of its plane (closed windows and series reads, Def. 11.4). A feed has no gaps (Theorem 11.5), or it reports `Gap`.

    **Definition 12.2 (replica).** A replica of graph `g` is a consumer-side copy `R` initialised from a snapshot at `r_s` and updated by applying each received patch with the guard of Def. 3.7. A patch whose revision is not the successor of the replica's, or whose before state does not match, is **rejected and reported**, never applied silently and never dropped without a counter.

    **Definition 12.3 (the seam).** The canvas consumes graphs through `GraphSource`: `open()` returns the graph space (each graph with role, descriptor, ontology, producing definition and inputs), `snapshot(graph)` returns the initial state, `subscribe(graph, sink)` delivers `patch`, `rejected` and `end` events in order, `telemetry` is an optional feed, `correspondences(graph)` optionally returns derived-to-source element records, `evaluate(definition)` returns `unavailable` or an output snapshot. Wire records are the schemas of `brainstorm/contracts/schemas/`, validated with the schema files themselves.

    ## 12.2 The canvas as a subscriber

    **Theorem 12.1 (replica correctness).** A replica that follows Definition 12.2 over a feed holds `G_r` after applying the patch of revision `r`, for every `r`, and holds a state equal to the engine's at that revision. A scoped replica (Def. 3.12) holds the scoped state.
    Status: **proved** from Theorems 11.5 and 11.7 and Def. 3.7, for a replica that implements Def. 12.2; the proof below is complete for the model and was read only. That the canvas implements Def. 12.2 is tested, with test evidence on the canvas side for the application and rejection rules (against a fake socket) and engine side for order, resume, gap and ahead-of-head. The end-to-end statement (engine output replayed by the canvas, replica digest equal to the engine digest at each revision) is an **obligation** (O12.1).

    *Proof.* The replica starts at `G_{r_s}` (the snapshot is the state at its revision, Theorem 11.7). Each accepted patch has the guard of Def. 3.7, so applying it gives the next state by Def. 3.8. By Theorem 11.5 the received patches are the log in order, so no revision is skipped. A rejected patch stops the replica from advancing and is visible, so a replica is never silently ahead of or behind the log. ∎

    **Proposition 12.2 (late join).** A client that joins late reads history over the snapshot and patch reads, and follows live over a stream. The two parts agree if the follow starts at the revision after the last read patch.
    Status: **proved** (Theorem 6.4). Protocol version 3 realizes it (12.5): one subscription yields a snapshot per graph with its sequence number and then every later event, and `after` resumes.

    **Theorem 12.3 (same entity across graphs).** Two elements in different graphs of a space with the same entity id denote the same entity, if both ids were produced through one identity domain and equal canonical components. An aggregate output has a new identity and never reuses a member's id. The canvas highlighting of "the same entity id in every graph" is therefore sound for entities and says nothing about aggregates.
    Status: the first sentence is **proved** (Theorem 1.4: one key gives one id). Its converse, that one entity has one id, holds only through `Rep`: after a merge a graph that was not evaluated again can still hold the element under the id that did not survive, and the highlight then misses it without being wrong. The second sentence is a rule for authors (ADR-094, Def. 5.9) and not a consequence of typing: this paper found no judgment in Ch. 5 (5.2) that refuses an emit whose identity is a member's id carried through an aggregate. Both remarks were added at the second reading of gate 1a.

    ## 12.3 Telemetry in a viewer

    The canvas holds telemetry in a ring of quantized frames per metric (8 bits per value, 61 ticks) and draws it as GPU channels (`canvas/README.md`, "Telemetry"). The paper's telemetry plane (Ch. 7) is keyed by `(g, k, p, τ)` and has no revisions, so a single slider that moves "revision and telemetry together" needs a defined alignment.

    **Definition 12.4 (time alignment).** `rev_of(τ)` is the largest revision of graph `g` whose logical time is at most `τ`, read from the commit records (Ch. 11, 11.5). The aligned element view at slider position `τ` is `view(k, rev_of(τ), [τ − w, τ))` (Def. 7.9).

    **Theorem 12.4 (alignment is well defined).** `rev_of` is monotone and defined for every `τ` at or after the first commit, and the aligned view is a function of `(G, M, τ)`.
    Status: **proved**.

    *Proof.* The *logical time of a revision* is the largest `λ` input among the inputs up to the cause of its commit. The commit record of Def. 3.8 does not hold it, so it is read from the input log, or from a field that the store adds (Ch. 11, 11.5). It is non-decreasing in the revision: causes follow the recorded order (F1 of Theorem 3.5), and `λ` is monotone per projection (Ch. 3, 3.4). The first form of this proof cited Def. 3.8 and Theorem 3.5, neither of which mentions logical time. The largest revision with `λ ≤ τ` therefore exists, and larger `τ` gives a revision at least as large. The view is a function of its arguments (Theorem 7.6). ∎

    The prototype does not have `rev_of`. The engine's revisions carry no commit time, so the canvas keeps one time axis for the whole space, puts telemetry frames at their window start and places revisions by order on the telemetry windows, and the timeline says so. The correct source of the map is `rev_of`, which the engine does not export (O12.5).

    ## 12.4 Definition graphs, editing and presentation in the canvas

    **Proposition 12.5 (definitions need no new mechanism).** Definitions are graphs of the catalog (Def. 2.1). A client can therefore subscribe to the catalog graph and to the definition modules exactly as to a data graph, and Theorem 12.1 applies. A change of a definition is a configuration edit (Ch. 9), proposed as a patch on `catalog` (on a draft, Ch. 4, 4.6).
    Status: **proved** (Def. 2.1, Theorem 12.1). **Implemented in the prototype.** The engine serves `catalog` like any data graph with the kernel as its ontology (`GET /graphs/catalog/snapshot`, `patches`, `ontology`), with definition ids as element ids and relationship ids `<source id>|<edge type>|<target id>`. The canvas reads it (modules, types, identity domains, projections, operators, ports, wires, rules with direction class) and its editor edits it through drafts. In the canvas measurement of `canvas/PERF.md` the `catalog` graph had 885 nodes. The engine README reports 1,048 entities and 1,969 relationships for the served catalog graph together with the kernel ontology, validated against the canvas schemas; the two counts are of different engine versions. The canvas also keeps a checker and editor model for the `ProjectionDefinition` of the contracts (`canvas/src/core/projection/*`), tested and not reachable from the UI.
    The projection editor shows what the checker accepts: its palette comes from `GET /palette`, derived from the kernel table and the engine's support list, and operators the engine cannot evaluate are listed disabled with the reason. Wiring is type checked in the client (`commands.canWire`) and again by the engine on every patch, with diagnostics by definition element id.

    **The shell and its state** (`canvas/README.md`). Fluent v9 components and tokens everywhere, with the themes `webLightTheme`, `webDarkTheme` and `teamsHighContrastTheme`. Custom drawing is limited to the WebGL view and the editor's SVG node canvas. One Zustand store holds plain, serializable state (summaries and UI state, never graphs) with Redux-style actions and a pure reducer. The mutable side (workspace, histories, GL panes, telemetry stores, the open draft) lives outside the store. `dispatch` runs the effects in order and then the reducer, and every server push becomes an action (`feed/patch`, `feed/telemetry`, `feed/telemetryReset`, `feed/resync`, `feed/connection`, `feed/rejected`). Patches and telemetry frames do not change the state themselves: effects report throttled summaries (at most every 100 ms for data, 250 ms for time), so frames never render React components. This is the reducer contract of Proposition 12.9 realized in the client.

    **Resume and caching.** The client reads the sequence number before the history, subscribes with `after = seq`, and resumes after the last sequence number it saw when the connection returns. Duplicates are dropped by revision (patches) and window time (telemetry). The shell shows "stale" while disconnected and "resynchronizing" after a gap. History is read from revision-addressed URLs, which are revalidated by a content tag (Proposition 12.10) and answer `304` when nothing changed, so the canvas needs no IndexedDB. The canvas README's measurement of a reload served from the browser cache without a request (`transferSize` 0) was taken when those URLs were marked immutable; it does not describe the current engine. The canvas README records runs against `sattle serve` on the lab fixtures and on a real capture (first view in 100 to 160 ms, live arrival on a paced replay, resume after killing the engine), which this paper did not repeat.

    **Accessibility and its limits.** The Elements tab shows the same graph as a Fluent DataGrid (nodes or edges, filterable, selection synced both ways) and the inspector lists connections as a Tree that navigates with Enter. This is the keyboard and screen reader alternative to the WebGL canvas, and the only view when WebGL2 is missing. Tests: axe on every view, the three themes and both dialogs in jsdom (structure, names, roles), keyboard tests of the element list, the connections tree and the shortcut dialog, and a script in Chrome for what jsdom cannot do (color contrast on every view in light, dark and high contrast, tab order, arrow keys on toolbar and tab list, dialog focus in and back to the opener, visible focus, the editor keyboard-only). Result of this paper's run at the canvas of evaluation 8 on the `estate` fixture (`paper/evidence/a11y-head8.txt`): 0 axe violations in all 32 page and dialog checks, all 12 keyboard and highlight checks pass, and every role color of the legend is present in the three themes. The script found, and the canvas fixed, button and card names that did not contain their visible text and a dialog that did not return focus. **Limits reported:** no screen reader pass (NVDA, VoiceOver); the WebGL canvas is one focusable group whose content is reached through the Elements list and the inspector; dragging in the editor has no pointer-free equivalent beyond Shift+arrows (move), `W` (connect) and `M` (region select); Chrome only.

    **Value roles: how a viewer maps health to color.** The engine serves roles as ontology data (Ch. 2, Def. 2.13); the viewer decides what they look like.
    - When a graph's ontology has roles for a property of nodes (or of edges), that property colors them by default, with no user action. Another property, or type colors, can be chosen per graph and separately for nodes and edges, each with its own control and legend. The override is not stored on the engine.
    - The roles map to Fluent status tokens (the success, warning and danger foregrounds; the neutral foregrounds for `neutral` and `unknown`) of the light or dark theme for the canvas and of the active theme for the legend. A value without a role is neutral.
    - Color is never the only cue. The legend lists value, count and the role with its glyph and word (`✓ ⚠ ✖ ?`, `○` for neutral). Labels carry the value and the glyph, danger edges are dashed, and the Elements list has a column with the value and the role word. In high contrast the glyph and word carry the role.
    - Colors follow patches and the timeline: a patch that changes the property recolors the element, and moving the time slider shows the state at that revision, legend counts included.
    - The canvas reads the `presentation` entries of the ontology and removes them before schema validation, because the contract schema `OntologyDefinition.schema.json` forbids extra fields on property definitions (the schema needs a `presentation` entry, which the engine README asks for).
    - Roles are edited in the Definitions tab ("Value roles") as `ValueRole` nodes under the property.
    - When a target has roles for several properties (the `health` graph: `Component.state`, `Signal.degraded`, `Signal.unhealthy`) the default keeps all of them: an element takes the worst role among the hinted properties it has (danger, warning, unknown, ok, neutral), its text names the property (`state: Healthy`), and the legend and the element list say so. The accessibility script checks this per theme on the `estate` engine: with no coloring chosen, a screenshot of the canvas must contain pixels of every role color of the legend, every element of a type with a hinted property must have a role, and the legend names each role.
    - Reported by the canvas README and not repeated here: on the `deploy-health` fixture, nodes and `Talks` edges are colored by `acme.deployhealth.state` with no user action, the legend lists `✓ Healthy (2)` and, at the unhealthy revisions, `✖ Unhealthy`, and the timeline of 10 points goes Healthy, both Unhealthy, one of each, Unhealthy again and back to Healthy.

    **Levels in the canvas.** A level is only useful to a reader who can see what it abstracts. Five behaviors serve that, all read from what the engine serves and none from names.
    - *The default source is engine output.* The app opens on the static files that `replay --out` writes for the synthetic estate (`npm run demo` regenerates them and refuses to write when a graph that should have edges has none; nothing in the directory is edited by hand). The graph space view shows the master with 47 nodes and 48 edges beside each projection with its edges: `serviceTree` 4 and 5, `capabilities` 4 and 4, `deploymentHealth` 3 and 2, `health` 40 and 45. Regenerated by this paper with the engine at evaluation 7, the 76 files are byte for byte the committed ones (`paper/evidence/demo-estate-head8.txt`). The engine at evaluation 9 writes 94 files for the same input, the 18 new ones being the references and the meter declarations of the nine graphs, with the same counts and the same state digest (`estate-head9.txt`); the committed default source is still the output of evaluation 7. The hand-authored demo is gone.
    - *Abstraction highlight.* Selecting an element highlights, in every graph, what it stands for below it and what it is part of above it. Forward lineage is followed level by level down to the master, reverse lineage up from it (Ch. 9). The accessibility script checks one case on the estate: selecting a lifted capability edge highlights 3 elements in `serviceTree` and 9 in the master (`a11y-head8.txt`). Lookups are one request per element and at most 400 per selection. The highlight is as complete as the engine's lineage: complete for row-level emits and, since protocol 3.12, for a `Group` without windows; an element emitted from a windowed aggregate has only its group key (Ch. 9, 9.5).
    - *Reference stubs.* A relationship that names an endpoint the graph does not hold (Def. 3.14) is drawn to a hollow stub with a dashed outline, labelled from the identity key the engine reports, and the inspector says why it is missing: `absent`, or in which other graph, with a link. Set A saw the master of the served newest capture with 589 stubs (563 Azure resources, 26 pods) and no edge left undrawn, the numbers of the replay (Ch. 3). The canvas reads the reasons from the head snapshot, which for that master is 39 MB; at most 5,000 stubs are drawn per graph. Protocol 3.12 serves the references alone and paged (`GET /graphs/{g}/references`, and `snapshot?references=false`), which the canvas at the canvas of evaluation 8 does not use.
    - *Supporting elements collapse.* In a graph whose ontology has value roles, elements that only support others are collapsed onto the node they support, as a count badge that expands. Which elements those are is decided from the ontology alone: a relationship type whose one end has no property with roles and whose other end has. Evidence of a component collapses, components and signals stay, and a graph without roles collapses nothing. On the served newest capture set A saw `health` with 11 components and 12 signals, 143 evidence elements as badges, and the propagated failure of Ch. 7: the warning-events signal, `db`, `api`, `frontend` and the workload `Unhealthy`, the other components `Healthy`.
    - *A notice for a graph without edges.* A projection with nodes, no edges and relationship types declared by its ontology says so on its canvas, so that missing lines are not mistaken for a rendering fault. On the estate that is `healthSignals` only.

    **Editing definitions from the palette.** The editor edits the draft of Ch. 4, 4.6. Operators, rule classes, aggregates and functions come from `GET /palette`. The palette also describes the declarations of a module (slots, node types, parameters, constraint kinds with the parts each uses, equivalence modes, field value kinds, rule parameters, `valueRoles`, `valueRoleSlot`), so the Definitions tab (row types with fields, constraints, equivalence policies, value roles) and the rule programs (class, reason, `putput`, `accept`, `onDelete`, create key, defaults and sets, governed types) need no client knowledge beyond the names of six connecting relationships (`constrains`, `onProperty`, `between`, `createKey`, `governs`, `valueKind`). An engine that does not describe a declaration gets a message and not a guess. A parameter port can be bound per graph in the space (`Binding.value`, typed by the port's value kind; the text shows `minReady: 4`). New projections are created from a dialog (name, output ontology, graph inputs, parameters, output graph). Text and graph stay in sync both ways: editing the Text tab sends `POST /check` and the difference between the draft graph and the parsed text becomes one draft patch, one undo step (Ch. 4, 4.6).
    - *Authored end to end.* A computed attribute was built once through the editor, with the same commands the UI sends, against the engine on the newest capture (66 pods, 15 services): a projection `podReady` over `master` with a parameter `minReady` bound to 1, a `Match`, a `Group` summing ready pods per service, and an `EmitEntity` of `health.Component` with `state = If(ready > minReady, "Healthy", "Unhealthy")`. The engine printed the module with no problem, a preview (partial scope, 500,824 of 579,175 rows) gave 13 components in 350 ms (7 `Healthy`, 6 `Unhealthy`), and the commit created catalog revision 14 and the graph in the space with 13 nodes. No step needed the Text tab. The engine's checker taught the editor four rules on the way: an optional property in a comparison needs `coalesce`, a property read after a group must be a group key, a create emit must set every required property, and a new `Group` comes with placeholders to delete. Reported by `canvas/README.md`, not repeated here.

    **Building every operator kind in the editor.** Every operator kind the engine has can be built in the graph editor without the Text tab: `Match`, `Filter`, `Map`, `Join`, `Group` with all aggregates, `Union` with its conflict policy, `Distinct`, `Values`, `Traverse`, `Series`, `Rollup`, `Threshold`, `Window`, `Retain`, `Fixpoint` (step operators and the `over` result), `EmitEntity`, `EmitRelationship` and `Link` with its policy (`canvas/README.md`). `Lift`, the twentieth kind, has a card of its own since the canvas of evaluation 11: two graph inputs (`edges`, `map`) and the relation as output, with everything it shows read from `/palette`. Two more walkthroughs rebuild `serviceDeps` of `acme.svc` and `capabilityView` of `acme.biz` with it: `lift-service-tree` (82 commands) and `lift-capabilities` (67 commands), both equal to the originals. That makes ten walkthroughs, strict on the estate 10 of 10 (from set A at the canvas of evaluation 11: a clean type check, 672 tests, the accessibility script passing, and the walkthroughs strict on the estate, 10 of 10). The table below is this paper's own run of the first eight at the canvas of evaluation 8. The evidence is eight *walkthroughs* (`canvas/tests/walkthroughs/*.json`), each a list of editor commands sent through `editor/do` to a draft on the engine, and a script that replays them in headless Chrome against the engine behind the app (`npm run walkthroughs`).

    *What the script checks for each walkthrough.* Every command is accepted. The engine reports no diagnostics beyond those the file allows. The projection the commands rebuild prints the same canonical text as the original and previews with the same digest. A projection written as text and applied through the Text path into a twin prints and previews the same as the one the commands built. For files marked `nonEmpty` or `atLeast` the preview has entities or relationships (at least that many for `atLeast`); this check is strict, and `--allow-empty` turns it into a note for an engine whose data are too small for some operators. Two rebuilds from scratch print the same canonical text as the originals, `deploymentHealth` (39 lines) and the health roll-up `health` (59 lines).

    *Results* (this paper's runs; the `estate` row at the canvas of evaluation 8 against the engine of evaluation 7, `paper/evidence/walkthroughs-head8.txt`; the real-capture row in evaluation 6, `walkthroughs-head6.txt`, not repeated):

    | Engine data | Passed | Previews (entities and relationships) |
    |---|---|---|
    | synthetic `estate`, strict | 8 of 8 (112, 32, 180, 10, 19, 60, 30 and 21 commands) | `deploymentHealth` 3 and 2; `kLoop` (`Fixpoint` closure) 0 and 26; `health` 40 and 45, its twin the same; `kRetain` 2; `kSeries` (`Series`, `Window`, `Threshold`) 4, its text twin 4; `kUnion` 10; `kValues` 2; `kLink` 0 and 0 |
    | newest real capture, `--allow-empty` | 8 of 8 | `deploymentHealth` 22 and 36; `kLoop` 0 and 33; `health` 347 and 353; `kRetain` 91; `kSeries` 3 (a note says 4 were expected, which is asserted on the `estate` only); `kUnion` 11; `kValues` 2 |

    A `Link` emits no element, so its walkthrough (`link-policy`) cannot count output in a graph. It asserts the identity effect that a preview reports (minted, aliased, merged and conflicts for the preview and for the live space, and the difference when the two are comparable): on the `estate` 5 more minted and 5 more merged identities than live, 0 aliased, 0 conflicts. On the real capture the effect is not comparable, because the preview saw part of the input. The walkthroughs for `Retain.age`, `Series.within` and `Window.width` type `36h` and `90s` into the properties panel with key events and check that the field shows what was typed and the engine holds the nanoseconds (a parameter with `quantity: "duration"` in the palette, Ch. 5). The health counts of the real-capture row were taken while a served capture used the short late horizon and the health view still took evidence outside the model; they are superseded by the replay figures of Ch. 7 (D31).

    *Aggregates per context.* The editor reads `GET /palette` `aggregates[].contexts` and disables, with the reason, an aggregate that the selected operator does not allow (Ch. 5, D26): a `Rollup` takes `count`, `sum`, `min`, `max` and, over a gauge, `mean`; a `Group` over data takes all ten.

    *Other editor features.* `Fixpoint` reads as a loop (a dotted region and a dashed arc, selected with `L`). Declared parameters with defaults show as port lines and are bound per graph. The New projection dialog takes `rows` inputs, parameter defaults, purpose and retained revisions. A problem that names only the projection leads with the operators the editor knows to be incomplete, each flagged on its card. Text applied over a draft that already holds graph edits keeps them. The `Window` half of `kSeries` previews no `Component` on `lab/fixtures` and previews data on the `estate`.

    **Refactoring from the UI.** Select operators or rules and move them into a new projection (extract), inline a projection, or move a rule. The engine plans a patch (`POST /drafts/{id}/refactor`), the dialog shows its size and the preserved-union note first, and Apply adds the patch to the draft as one undoable step. Engine refusals (`SharedOperator`, `DependsOnUnmoved`, and the others of Ch. 5, 5.7) are shown as they come. Reported by the canvas README and not repeated here: extract of a lens rule gives a patch with two warnings (the new projection emits the relationship and not its endpoint entities), extracting `capabilities` and `tree` together answers `UpdateWithoutCreate`, inline and move rule apply cleanly, and undo of all three restores the module. The preservation property of Proposition 5.7 is the engine's, checked by its tests and not by the canvas.

    ## 12.5 Protocol version 3 as the feed, and the mismatches of the first export

    **Definition 12.7 (ordered event stream).** The server owns the committed state and emits one stream of events, each with a server-wide sequence number `seq` that increases by one (`snapshot`, `patch`, `telemetry`, `activation`, `draft`, `end`, `error`; `time` is unsequenced). A subscription names graphs and optionally `after = seq`. Without `after` it receives one snapshot per graph, current as of its `seq`, and then every later event. With `after` it receives the later events, or, when `after` is older than the retained events (the last 20,000 by default, `serve --retain-events`, D30; the patches of a graph are kept for the last 2,000 revisions by default, `--retain-patches`, and an older revision answers `410 Gap`), a `gap` and then snapshots. A client ahead of the server gets `AheadOfHead`. A subscription that has more than 10,000 queued messages is closed with `SlowConsumer`.

    **Proposition 12.9 (reducer contract).** A client that applies a `patch` of graph `g` iff `patch.from` equals its local head of `g`, and otherwise resubscribes with `after` equal to the last `seq` it applied, holds for every graph a state equal to the engine's state at some revision, and never skips or repeats a revision.
    Status: **proved** from Theorem 12.1: a client that applies a patch only at its own head is a replica in the sense of Def. 12.2 per graph, and a resubscription with `after` restarts the feed at the next event, which Theorem 11.5 makes gap-free or reports as a `gap`. Read only. Tested: the stream's order, resume, gap and ahead-of-head on the engine; on the client, reconnect, resume after the last sequence number and duplicate dropping by revision (patches) and window time (telemetry) against a fake socket, and one live exercise (killing and restarting the engine), recorded in `canvas/README.md`. The `gap`, `AheadOfHead`, `SlowConsumer` and `activation` messages are tested against a fake socket only.

    **Proposition 12.10 (resources, tags and caching).** (a) Within one run of one store, a response addressed by a revision (`GET /graphs/{g}/snapshot?revision=r`, `patches?after=a&to=b` with `b` not above the head, `document?revision=r`) is a function of its URL, and a response addressed by a digest (`GET /releases/{digest}`, its `text` and `diff`) is a function of the digest. (b) A revision number, a catalog revision and a sequence number are unique only within one run of one store: two runs with different definitions reach the same catalog revision, and two runs can have the same graph revision with different content. Therefore only resources whose URL contains a content digest are `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable`; every other cached resource is `no-cache` and is revalidated; and **every** `ETag` is the first 32 hexadecimal digits of SHA-256 over the media type, a newline and the body bytes, nothing else going into it. Equal bytes have equal tags in any run and after a restart from a store, different bytes never share a tag, `If-None-Match` with a tag the server does not know answers `200` with the body and the current tag, and a matching tag answers `304`.
    Status: (a) **proved** from the immutability of history (Theorem 3.2) and the content addressing of releases (Theorem 2.4); (b) is a **definition of the engine's behavior** with a **tested** consequence (tested: two hosts whose definitions differ in one value role answer different tags for the ontology at the same catalog revision; the same content has the same tag after a restart from a store and in another host instance; a stale `If-None-Match` answers the body and a matching one answers `304` without building the body while nothing changed; the tag depends on the media type and on nothing but the content). **D29 (found in set A, fixed).** Tags used to be built from a graph name and a revision (`"onto-health-16"`), and revision-addressed responses were marked immutable, which is true within one run only. After a rebuild with changed definitions a browser revalidated the ontology with its old tag, was told `304` and kept an ontology without value roles. Part (b) is the rule that replaced it (protocol 3.9).

    The first engine export differed from the canvas contract in eight points (D16). Each is listed with the resolution this paper gives and its state in the prototype:

    | Mismatch | Paper's resolution | State |
    |---|---|---|
    | Revisions are integer positions on the engine, decimal strings `{value}` on the wire | Def. 3.8 and Rule 1.2 | resolved: decimal strings on the wire, the canvas treats them as ordered integers |
    | Definition graphs have no counterpart in the canvas contract | Proposition 12.5 | resolved: `catalog` is served and read (12.4) |
    | Telemetry tick k is aligned with revision k, engine telemetry is keyed by window start | Def. 12.4, `rev_of` | **open.** Every batch carries `windowStart`, the canvas keeps one shared time axis and places a revision at its commit time when the source gives one (`Patch.time`). The engine's revisions carry no commit time, so revisions are placed by order on the telemetry windows and the timeline says so. `rev_of` is not exported (O12.5) |
    | Meters take min and max from the replayed data, thresholds are not exported | thresholds belong to the definition graph (Def. 5.11) | thresholds resolved: `GET /graphs/{g}/meters` lists them from the definitions; min and max still come from the retained windows |
    | Type and property ids are definition names, qualified when two modules share a name | Def. 2.3 path ids | **open** |
    | No correspondences are exported | provenance record (Def. 9.9) | resolved in another form: `GET /graphs/{g}/lineage` (Ch. 9, 9.5), which the canvas follows for the abstraction highlight (12.4) |
    | `evaluate(definition)` has no engine endpoint | configuration edit (Ch. 9) | resolved: `POST /check`, drafts, `POST /drafts/{id}/preview`, `POST /drafts/{id}/commit` |
    | No snapshot-then-follow handshake on one connection | Proposition 12.2 | resolved by Def. 12.7 |

    Protocol defects of the prototype that the canvas README records as open, none of them a property of the design: an `Unprintable` answer for a `Match` with no pattern node names the projection and not the `Match`, so the editor cannot flag the card; a constraint with a non-default name prints as a named constraint and breaks the module text, so the canvas names constraints by the default; the kernel ontology uses `valueKind: "any"`; revisions carry no commit time. `POST /graphs/{g}/edits` and the editability endpoint are not used by the canvas (O12.4). Relationships with a missing endpoint, which the first canvas counted in the status bar and did not draw, are drawn as reference stubs (12.4).

    ## 12.6 Documents as projections

    A document projection is a configured transformation from graph facts to an ordered list of structured blocks. The engine realizes it **without a kernel change**: a document is a derived graph whose output ontology is `sattle.doc`, and any projection can produce one (protocol 3.4, `engine/definitions/sattle.doc.sattle`).

    **Definition 12.5 (document, block, patch).** A document is `D = (order, blocks)` with `order` a list of block ids without repetition and `blocks` a map from block id to block content (heading, paragraph, table with keyed rows, list, code). A document has its own integer revision, a vector of source graph revisions, the projection version and a completeness flag `Complete` or `ExplicitlyTruncated`. A document patch is `P = (E, order_before, order_after, sources_before, sources_after)` with `E` a set of block edits `(id, before, after)` with distinct ids. Orders and edits are independent.
    **Block identity.** The id of a block is derived from the rule id and the source identity or group key, never from the row position or a label: `bid = base32(H(JCS([rule, group key])))`. A table row has a row key from the declared row identity fields. Row order must be total, so templates append a stable identity tie-breaker (`ARCHITECTURE.md`).

    **Definition 12.8 (document graph, engine realization).** The ontology `sattle.doc` has one entity type `Block { kind, order, text?, level?, a?, b?, c?, d?, n? }` and the identity domain `block(rule, key)`, so the block id is derived from the rule and the source key. `kind` is `heading` (`level` 1 to 6), `paragraph`, `item`, `stat` (`text` is the label, `n` the value), `header` or `row` (cells `a` to `d`, or `n` instead of `d`); consecutive `header` and `row` blocks form one table. `order` is a text sort key, and blocks are ordered by `order` (ordinal) and then by id, so the order is total. `GET /graphs/{g}/document[?revision=r][&format=markdown|json]` renders the graph at the head or at a revision, and the graph entry in `GET /space` carries `document: true`. A graph that is not a document answers `404 NotADocument`.

    **Differences from Def. 12.5 (D24).** The document is the graph, so its revision is the graph's revision, a document patch is a graph patch (inversion is Theorem 4.3 and not the separate statement of Theorem 12.7), and `order` is a property of each block and not a list independent of the blocks. The source vector of Def. 12.5 is not part of the rendered document. The completeness flag and `ExplicitlyTruncated` are not built, so a document over an unbounded graph has no declared truncation.

    **Theorem 12.6 (stable block identity, T14).** Block ids are invariant under reordering of the output, under change of a label and under change of row position. Two blocks have the same id iff the rule id and the group key are equal, unless `H` has a collision.
    Status: **proved** (Theorem 1.1: `bid` is a function of canonical bytes of `[rule, key]` and of nothing else). The engine's identity domain `block(rule, key)` has exactly these components.

    **Theorem 12.7 (document patch inversion, T14).** If `apply(P, D)` is defined then `apply(inverse(P), apply(P, D)) = D`, where `inverse(P)` swaps before and after in every edit, in the orders and in the source vectors, and swaps the revisions. A patch applied to a document at a different revision, order or content is rejected.
    Status: **proved** on the model of Def. 12.5. The engine realizes documents as graphs, where the statement is Theorem 4.3 for graph patches.

    *Proof.* Edits are keyed by distinct block ids and orders are separate lists, so `apply` is the same as the guarded edit of Def. 3.7 on the map `blocks` and a replacement of the list `order`, whose guards are the exact base. The inverse has the matching guards on the result, so the argument of Theorem 4.3 applies to each of the two components. A different base fails a guard. ∎

    **Theorem 12.8 (rendering is a function, escaping).** A renderer maps a document snapshot to bytes and is a function of the snapshot and the pinned renderer version. Text from graph data is escaped, so it cannot create document structure.
    Status: **function: proved by definition, and tested. Escaping: tested by four examples, not a property** (O12.3). The renderer writes LF line endings and one final newline, escapes a backslash before ``\ ` * _ { } [ ] < > ( ) # + - . ! | ~`` in data text, and turns a line break into a space. Tests: four escape cases (plain text, a table pipe, markdown heading and link syntax, a line break); blocks are rendered in the order given by `order` and then id, and the order in which the graph was built does not matter; a document is the same bytes at every revision in two replays of the same input. The contract oracle's single hostile paragraph is not a property either.

    **Proposition 12.11 (a document digest per revision).** The digest of a document at revision `r` is `sha256` of the rendered Markdown bytes. Equal graph states give equal documents and equal digests, and the digest changes when a rendered block changes. `X-Document-Digest` carries it, the ETag is the content tag of Proposition 12.10 (a digest of the body and its media type, so the tag differs between formats and is equal after a restart), and both `GET ...?revision=r` and the head response are `no-cache` and revalidated, the head response with the `Content-Location` of the revision URL.
    Status: **proved** from Theorem 12.8 (function), **tested by example and measured**. On the fixture `deploy-health` (a synthetic shop, `paper/evidence/document-head.txt`) the report has two rows at the head, and earlier revisions show the Deployment `db` `Unhealthy` with 0 ready pods at revision 5, `web` with 2 ready pods at revision 3, and an empty table at revision 1; the digests differ per revision (`DocumentTests`: the document changes when a Deployment turns `Unhealthy`). On the first real capture the document of the report (`deploymentReport`, 26 blocks: 1 heading, 2 stat, 1 header, 22 row, 29 lines) has the same digest `sha256:7d2b8be6…f2690` at chunk size 1, where the graph is at revision 60, and at chunk size 100,000, where it is at revision 39. The document is a function of the graph state and not of the revision numbers. The text of that document is not kept because it names workloads of the lab cluster.

    **Worked example** (`engine/definitions/acme.report.sattle`, graph `deploymentReport` over `deploymentHealth`). A heading, two `stat` blocks (deployments and unhealthy deployments, by `Group`), a table header, and one `row` block per Deployment keyed by cluster, namespace and name. The projection is ordinary definition text: `Values` for the title and the header, `Group` for the totals, `EmitEntity` of `doc.Block` with `identity doc.block(rule, key)`. Nothing in the engine knows the report; the engine knows the ontology `sattle.doc` and one renderer.

    **Remarks and gaps.**
    - The kernel needs no document sink: the design amendment O12.2 is resolved by an ontology and one generic renderer with fixed block kinds. A renderer for another block kind or another format (JSON is the second) is an engine release.
    - A document over an unbounded graph needs a finite selection or an explicit truncation (`ExplicitlyTruncated`). The engine has neither, so a large graph renders in full.
    - Markdown byte editing and write-back from documents to graphs are not implied. A document patch is a patch of the graph.
    - Documents follow graph feeds, so a document is itself a derived, versioned output with its own log, and Theorem 11.5 applies to its subscribers.
    - **Document view in the canvas**. A graph whose entry in `/space` has `"document": true` shows in the data view as a page with the tabs Document and Graph. The page is the Markdown of `GET /graphs/{g}/document?revision=N` for the revision the timeline shows, so scrubbing changes the document, and at the head it follows the event stream (the graph's version changes and the view asks again with `If-None-Match`, keeping its copy on `304`). The canvas reads the fixed Markdown subset the engine writes (headings, lists, tables, escaped data text) and draws it with Fluent `Title`, `Text` and `Table`.
      - *Tested* (unit and component tests, `canvas-tests-head8.txt`): the example report parses to blocks, the graph entry says whether it is a document, a revision is fetched once and then served from the cache, the head revalidates with its tag and keeps its copy on `304`, the view follows the timeline and a patch, an empty revision says so, a failing engine gives a message, and axe finds no violation in jsdom. In Chrome the accessibility script finds 0 violations on the document view in three themes (`a11y-head8.txt`).
      - *Follows the live stream* (`npm run document-live`, `document-live-head8.txt`): against an engine that plays `lab/fixtures` at `--speed 2`, with the page opened once and never reloaded, `deploymentReport` passes through revisions 2 and 3 and ends at revision 3 with the digest that `GET /graphs/{g}/document` gives (revisions 1, 2 and 3 in the run of evaluation 6, `document-live-head6.txt`; which revisions the page sees depends on when it opens).
      - *Not tested:* the view shows the first 19 characters of the revision's digest in a badge; whether it equals the digest the engine reports at that revision is not compared (O12.1).

    ## 12.7 Delta to iteration 0.1

    - **Canvas contract.** The `GraphSource` seam is the consumer contract. Its `ProjectionDefinition` is replaced by catalog graph subscription (Proposition 12.5), which the prototype implements.
    - **Protocol version 3.** The ordered event stream with resume, revision-addressed resources revalidated by content tags, drafts, releases, preview and refactoring are not in the contracts. Def. 12.7 and Propositions 12.9 and 12.10 record them.
    - **Telemetry in the viewer.** Alignment by `rev_of` (Def. 12.4) replaces "tick k is revision k".
    - **Documents.** Block ids by rule and key. In the engine the order is a property of the block and the document is a graph of the ontology `sattle.doc` (Def. 12.8); no kernel sink was needed (O12.2 resolved).
    - **Findings 32 to 34.** The oracle's document tests are finite examples. T14 is now proved on the model and still tested only by the finite oracle.

    ## 12.8 Obligations

    | Id | Statement | Discharged by |
    |---|---|---|
    | O12.1 | Theorem 12.1 end to end | run the canvas on `sattle replay --out` files and `sattle serve`, compare replica digest with the engine's state digest at each revision |
    | O12.2 | A document sink in the kernel | resolved differently: an ontology `sattle.doc` and one renderer, no kernel change (Def. 12.8) |
    | O12.3 | Theorem 12.8 escaping as a property (four examples exist) | property test over generated hostile strings for each renderer, and for `format=json` |
    | O12.4 | Configuration-edit proposals in the canvas for lens views and data edits | the canvas calls `POST /graphs/{g}/edits` and the editability endpoint (not built in the canvas) |
    | O12.5 | Time alignment of one slider | export `rev_of` and use it in the canvas |
    | O12.6 | Restore exercised live from the canvas, and refactoring repeated on the health module (the canvas README records extract, inline and move on the capability module) | run the editor on the health module and record the result |
    | O12.7 | Truncation of a document over an unbounded graph (the canvas document view is built) | an `ExplicitlyTruncated` flag or a selection limit; the canvas compares the digest it shows with the engine's |


## Ch. 13 Performance model and benchmarks

The whole chapter as it stood before the reader rewrite (2026-11-12). The rewrite keeps the target, definitions, theorems, hypotheses, method and results; file names, evaluation numbers, the treatment of the contract gaps P-a to P-g, the delta, the deviations as a list and the obligations are kept only here.

    # Ch. 13 Performance model and benchmarks

    **Claim.** Performance targets are hypotheses H1 to H14, each with a benchmark and a refutation rule. A status is given by a written decision rule and belongs to a named configuration: a store, a machine and the recorded load.
    **Status.** Measured once per figure: Q14, H1 to H4, H6, H9, H12, and a 240,000-pair estate. Measured in ten runs each, on a machine under load (load average 14 to 115, 239 once; no machine without other load was available): H5, H7, H8, H10. By the rule of 13.6, **H5 (engine part, memory and SQLite), H8 (hot plane, head revision) and H10 (restart from a SQLite journal of 1.2 and 4.1 hours of repeated capture, with and without a state seal) are `supported` for those configurations**. **H7 has no status for a real account**; on the Cosmos emulator one set of runs is `refuted` and a later one `inconclusive`. H9 is met in one run (337 MB). The window close at the estate size is above the target of H4 and H12 in one run. Not measured: H11, H13, H14 (`Join` is built and has no cost measurement), B15, B16. Nothing here is on the reference hardware of Def. 13.4. The figures are in 13.7 and Ch. 14 (14.7). Claim 13.5 (hot read cost) is proved as counts on the layout of Ch. 11 (11.5); the engine's cost is benchmark B6.
    **Gate.** 1c (efficient). The hypotheses H1 to H14 were written before the gate: they name a reference estate of 750,000 entities, Azure hardware and Cosmos DB, and none is a budget per node, edge or telemetry point at 1 million master nodes. They stay as the record against that estate; 13.0 states the target of the gate, leaves a marked place for the budgets and says which hypotheses carry over.
    Uses: Ch. 3 (revision log), Ch. 4 (patch size), Ch. 7 (closed windows, crossing operators), Ch. 11 (stores, estate estimate, hot graph, experiments E1 to E20).
    Forward references: Ch. 5, 6, 9 for operators, T5, T6 and configuration edits (written).
    Used by: Ch. 14.
    Sources: [R01] section 4.2 (P-a to P-g), ADR-093; [R02] section 5, section 6; `ARCHITECTURE.md:3,51,75`; `OPEN-WORK.md` items on bounded memory and load tests.

    ## 13.0 The target of gate 1c, and what the hypotheses below are

    **The target.** Gate 1c of the research plan is: a budget per node, per edge and per telemetry point, met at 1 million master nodes on one machine, with persistence that does not depend on one store and that scales.

    **The budgets are not stated yet.** They come from the investigation `paper/research/04-compute-and-memory-efficiency.md` when it is finished. This table is the place for them.

    | Per | Memory | Work per change | Persisted bytes |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | master node | not stated | not stated | not stated |
    | master edge | not stated | not stated | not stated |
    | telemetry point | not stated | not stated | not stated |

    **What H1 to H14 are.** They were written before the gate, against a reference estate of about 750,000 entities, a node of 8 vCPU and 32 GB, and Cosmos DB as the store (Def. 13.1 to 13.4, [R02]). They and their measurements stay unchanged as the record against that estate. None of them is a budget per element.

    | Hypothesis | Carries over to gate 1c | Why |
    |---|---|---|
    | H1 structural ingest, H2 telemetry ingest | yes, to be measured again at 1 million master nodes | rates of the engine, independent of the store |
    | H3 commit latency | not as stated | it names Cosmos DB. Under the gate it is a commit latency per backend, and no backend is the target |
    | H4 projection latency per hop, H12 telemetry to property | yes | engine figures. Already above target at 240,000 pairs (13.7) |
    | H5 source to graph | the engine part yes, the store part per backend | `supported` on memory and SQLite at the scale of the lab capture only |
    | H6 hot reads, H8 element view | yes | engine figures. The sizes they name are those of the old estate |
    | H7 durable reads | not as stated | it names the Cosmos `state` container. It becomes a read latency per backend |
    | H9 memory | superseded | it bounds the hot graph only (about 100 bytes per element, met in one run) and not the whole engine, which holds 5.4 GB on the estate run. The budgets replace it |
    | H10 cold start | yes | measured on SQLite, with nothing specific to a store |
    | H11 edit replay, H14 join cost | yes | engine figures, not measured |
    | H13 materialization lag | no | it measures a watermark that no adapter has; it was specific to the Cosmos design |

    ## 13.1 What "very high performance" means here

    ADR-093 states "low maintenance and broad scale" as a goal and leaves limits open. This chapter turns the goal into **falsifiable hypotheses**, each with a named benchmark and a refutation rule. A hypothesis is neither a claim nor a result. Section 13.7 reports what the first prototype measured against them.
