    ## 13.2 Definitions

    **Definition 13.1 (workload model).** `W = (E, ρ, Σ, f, d)` with
    - `E`: estate size (entities, relationships, projections);
    - `ρ`: source event rate per family (ARG pages and changes, Kubernetes watch, Hubble flows, network flow logs, OTel metrics);
    - `Σ`: number of telemetry series and their resolution;
    - `f`: the fraction of inputs that change graph data;
    - `d`: stack depth, the longest path from the master in the graph space.
    The reference workload is the estimate of Ch. 11 section 11.3 (all inputs assumptions). The lab capture (`lab/`) replaces assumptions with measurements (E5, E20).

    **Definition 13.2 (metrics).**

    | Id | Metric | Unit |
    |---|---|---|
    | M1 | sustained structural ingest before backpressure | records/s per graph space |
    | M2 | telemetry append rate | samples/s per node |
    | M3 | commit latency: evaluated commit record to durable | ms, percentiles |
    | M4 | freshness latency per hop: master commit to derived commit | s, percentiles |
    | M5 | source-to-graph latency: engine receipt to master revision | s, percentiles |
    | M6 | telemetry-to-derived-property latency: closing input to derived commit | s, percentiles |
    | M7 | read latency: hot point, hot adjacency, hot k-hop, durable point, durable scan | µs, ms |
    | M8 | element view latency (Def. 7.9) | ms |
    | M9 | memory per entity, relationship and series point; storage per revision | bytes |
    | M10 | cold start: load seal and replay the log tail | s |
    | M11 | configuration edit replay time | s |
    | M12 | materialization lag: commit to watermark (Ch. 11) | s |

    **Definition 13.3 (tier).** A tier is `(scale factor over the reference workload, reference hardware, target table)`. Tier 1 is the reference workload (factor 1). Tiers at factor 4 and 16 are used by B9 only (E15). Targets exist for tier 1 only. Targets for other tiers are set after tier-1 results.

    **Definition 13.4 (reference hardware).** One engine node of 8 vCPU and 32 GB per graph space (assumption). A Cosmos DB account with provisioned autoscale throughput on the master log (11.4). The exact Azure SKUs, regions, throughput settings and consistency level are fixed at the first run and written into every result file. **No result is valid without them.**

    **Definition 13.5 (hypothesis).** `Hn = (target, benchmark Bn, refutation rule)`. Status is one of `hypothesis`, `supported`, `refuted`, `inconclusive` (13.6).

    **Definition 13.6 (per-input cost).** For one accepted structural input:

    `cost(input) = parse + identity lookup + Σ_ops cost_op(Δ) + commit / groupSize`

    with `cost_op` from Fact 11.1, identity lookup `O(1)` on the binding set, and `commit` one store batch per group (Ch. 11).

    ## 13.3 Cost theorems

    **Theorem 13.1 (join cost is output-sensitive).** Let `a` and `b` be indexed by the join key, `d_a` and `d_b` the maximal numbers of rows of the integrals sharing a key. One incremental join step on changes `Δa, Δb` costs `|Δa| + |Δb|` index lookups plus the number of produced rows, and the number of produced rows is at most `|Δa|·d_b + |Δb|·d_a + |Δa|·|Δb|`.
    Status: **proved**, conditional on the bilinear incrementalization rule of [budiu2023] (T5, Ch. 6): `(a ⋈ b)^Δ = Δa ⋈ Δb + I(a) ⋈ Δb + Δa ⋈ I(b)`.

    *Proof.* Each of the three terms is a join between a delta and an indexed set. A hash or ordered index finds the matching rows of the other side with one lookup per delta row, and enumerating the matches costs their number. For a delta row `y` with key `κ`, the number of matches in `I(a)` is `deg_a(κ) ≤ d_a`. Summing gives the stated bounds. ∎

    A change that touches a hub of degree `d` costs `Θ(d)` produced rows (Fact 11.2), which is why H14 tests the proportionality.

    **Proposition 13.2 (group commit amortization).** Let a store batch of `n` revisions cost `C(n) = α + β·n`, with `α` the per-batch overhead and `β` the per-revision payload cost, for `n ≤ n_max = min(100, ⌊2 MB / revision size⌋)` (S1, Ch. 11). Then the cost per revision `β + α/n` decreases strictly in `n` and is smallest at `n_max`. The first revision of a group waits for the group to fill or a flush deadline, so group size trades latency for cost.
    Status: **model** (by arithmetic). `α, β` are hypotheses, measured in E1 and E2.

    **Theorem 13.3 (tandem pipeline).** Consider stages `1 … m` (acceptance, evaluation, commit, each projection hop) with service rates `μ_i` records per second. With bounded queues that block their producers, the sustained throughput is at most `min_i μ_i`. With unbounded queues, a stage with arrival rate `λ_i > μ_i` accumulates backlog at rate at least `λ_i − μ_i`. A projection that processes its inputs sequentially (ADR-017) has `μ ≤ 1 / s̄` where `s̄` is its mean service time per record or group.
    Status: **proved**.

    *Proof.* Flow conservation: the backlog of stage `i` changes at `λ_i − (departure rate) ≥ λ_i − μ_i`. With blocking queues a producer cannot exceed the consumer's departure rate, so every stage runs at most at the slowest rate. Sequential processing serves one record or group at a time, so the departure rate is at most the reciprocal of the mean service time. ∎

    Consequence for H1: 5,000 records per second through one sequential evaluator needs a mean service time of at most 200 µs per record, commit amortization included (arithmetic on the target). Parallelism comes from many graphs (sharding by tenant and cluster, Ch. 11), not from within one projection (gap P-a).

    **Theorem 13.4 (latency budget composition).** Let `L = X₁ + … + X_d` with `P(X_j > q_j) ≤ ε_j`. Then `P(L > q₁ + … + q_d) ≤ ε₁ + … + ε_d`. Hence, for a stack of `d` hops, if every hop meets its budget `q` at quantile `1 − 0.01/d`, then `d·q` is an end-to-end budget at p99, and if every hop meets `q` only at p99, then `d·q` holds only at the `1 − 0.01·d` quantile.
    Status: **proved**.

    *Proof.* If `L > Σ q_j` then at least one `X_j > q_j` (otherwise the sum is at most `Σ q_j`). The union bound gives the claim. ∎

    B4 reports the per-hop histograms and the end-to-end percentile directly. Theorem 13.4 says which per-hop quantile a stack depth requires.

    **Claim 13.5 (hot read cost).** For the hot layout of Ch. 11 (11.5): dense handles from an intern table, one column per property in fixed segments, adjacency as a base CSR plus an append-only delta log per vertex, each entry with a revision interval.
    - (a) A read of one property of an element by its handle at the published base costs two array accesses, a segment and an offset. From an id it costs one intern lookup first: a hash of the id and an expected constant number of probes in a table of bounded load.
    - (b) A read at a pinned revision inspects in addition the delta entries of that element that are not yet merged into the base: `O(1 + ℓ_k)` for `ℓ_k` such entries.
    - (c) A one-hop adjacency scan of a vertex `v` at a pinned revision visits `deg_base(v) + L_v` entries, each tested against the pinned revision in constant time, where `deg_base(v)` is the length of the vertex's slice of the base CSR and `L_v` the length of its delta log. With the delta log kept below a merge threshold `θ` (a parameter), that is `O(deg(v) + θ)`.
    Status: (a) to (c) are **proved** as counts on the layout of 11.9, restated. They are statements about a design. That the engine's hot graph has this layout, and what the constants are, is a hypothesis of gate 1c, settled by measurement and not by proof: benchmark **B6** (point reads by handle and by id at 10⁴, 10⁵ and 10⁶ elements, where the time per read must not grow with the size; one-hop scans over vertex degrees from 1 to 10⁵ and delta logs from 0 to `θ`, where the time must be linear in `deg_base(v) + L_v`). The first form said that a hot point read is `O(1)` without conditions; (a) and (b) say for which read. Counterexample to the first form: an element edited `ℓ` times since the last merge, read at a revision before those edits, costs `ℓ` entry tests, which no constant bounds unless a merge threshold applies per element.

    *Proof.* (a) A handle is a dense integer (interning, 11.9). A column lives in fixed segments of 64 K elements, so the value of handle `i` is at segment `⌊i / 2¹⁶⌋`, offset `i mod 2¹⁶`: two accesses. The intern table maps an id to its handle by hashing; with bounded load the expected number of probes is constant, and hashing reads the id once. (b) Base arrays are immutable once published, and a reader at revision `r` filters the delta entries by `from ≤ r < to` (11.9): the entries of the element are inspected once each. (c) The base CSR is sorted by `(vertex, traversal direction, type)`, so the edges of `v` are one contiguous slice, found by two offsets; each entry is tested once. The delta log of `v` is scanned once. ∎

    ## 13.5 How the contract limits of [R01] 4.2 are treated

    | Gap | Issue | Treatment in the paper | Tested by |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | P-a | strictly sequential processing per projection caps throughput, stacked projections add latency | accepted. Theorem 13.3, Theorem 13.4, shard by `(tenant, cluster)` | H1, H4 |
    | P-b | patches carry full before and after states, so a one-property change costs `O(entity size)` twice | the logical model keeps full states (guards need `b`, Ch. 4). The physical encoding may store property-level differences if decoding yields the same canonical states. Telemetry never enters this path (Theorem 7.1) | B1 sweeps entity size |
    | P-c | one evaluation request per input, per-input commit | evaluate in batches and commit in groups with revision boundaries preserved (Proposition 13.2, Ch. 11). Batch invariance is T6 (Ch. 6) | H1, H3 |
    | P-d | per-element history keeps up to `N` original payloads | the evidence head holds input ids (Ch. 11). Bytes stay in the input log under its retention (Ch. 9) | H9 |
    | P-e | global relationship uniqueness needs a cross-partition reservation | not needed. Theorem 11.8 | E12 |
    | P-f | configuration edits pause the projection and replay retained inputs | cost model `O(N · elements)`, H11 | B11 |
    | P-g | string ids and polymorphic JSON at every boundary | interning and columns inside the engine (Ch. 11, 11.5). Strings and JSON at the boundary only | H6, H9 |

    ## 13.6 Method

    - **Determinism of the harness.** Fixed seeds for generators, input from the recorded lab capture where possible, published harness and result files in the repository.
    - **Runs.** A warm-up phase, then at least 10 independent runs per configuration. Report median, p50, p99 and the 95% confidence interval of the median across runs, and the variance.
    - **Decision rule.** A latency or size target is *supported* iff the reported statistic is at or below the target in every run, *refuted* iff the median run is above the target, otherwise *inconclusive*. A throughput target is supported iff the lower bound of the 95% interval is at or above the target.
    - **Scope of a status.** The rule above says nothing about the machine, and Def. 13.4 names a reference that none of the runs so far used. A status is therefore stated for a *configuration*: a store, a machine and the recorded load. The rule is applied to the runs of one configuration, and the status carries its name ("`supported` on SQLite on the lab laptop under load"). It is the status of the hypothesis itself only where the hypothesis names no store (H5 and H10 name none; H7 names the Cosmos `state` container, H3 names Cosmos). For an upper-bound latency target, load on the machine can only raise latencies, so a pass under load holds a fortiori for that configuration, while a failure under load is weak evidence. A `refuted` on the emulator is reported as such and does not refute the hypothesis for a real account. A machine with no other load was not available for any of the ten-run figures, and the threat stays in Ch. 14 (14.9).
    - **Environment record.** Def. 13.4 plus engine commit, .NET version, Cosmos and ADX settings, and the workload parameters actually used.
    - **Microbenchmarks** use BenchmarkDotNet. System benchmarks use the harness and the lab capture.
    - **Separation of causes.** B3 runs against a real Cosmos account (the emulator has no RU accounting, Ch. 11). Hot-plane benchmarks run with the store stubbed, so that store effects do not enter H4, H6, H8.

    ## 13.8 Delta to iteration 0.1

    - ADR-093 is supplemented by H1 to H14. The oracle and `VERIFICATION.md` remain correctness evidence only.
    - `EvaluationInput` carries one input per request. A batched evaluation input is required (P-c).
    - `RetainedInput.OriginalBytes` per element is replaced by input ids on the element and bytes in the input log (P-d).
    - Numeric targets name hardware and store configuration (Def. 13.4). The research text names a hardware class only (R02 section 5); this paper adds the rule that the SKU is fixed at the first run.

    ## 13.9 Deviations found by the prototype

    Ids refer to Ch. 14 (14.8).

    - **D14 (method).** The benchmark uses Stopwatch and GC counters, not BenchmarkDotNet, and each figure is one run (ten for H5, H7, H8, H10) on an Apple M1 Max, not the reference hardware of Def. 13.4 (`engine/PERF.md`). The ten-run figures were taken at a load average of 14 to 115 (239 during one set of three restarts), so latencies are pessimistic and the spread is wide; no machine with no other load was available. The statuses of 13.7 follow the rule of 13.6 with its scope clause.
    - **Workload.** The synthetic workload has 1,000 pods, 5,000 pod pairs and 100 services. The reference estate has about 240,000 flow pairs.
    - **The health model in H5 and H10 at evaluation 6.** The benchmarks load the tracked definitions and not the local space overlay that `replay` and `serve` apply to a real capture. Since evaluation 6 the tracked space binds the health model to invented names, so on the real capture the health topology and state graphs are empty in a benchmark and the signals are 3 and not 12 (`paper/evidence/bench-overlay-head6.txt`: the same capture replayed without and with the overlay). At evaluation 5 the tracked space carried the capture's names and the benchmarks ran the whole model. `engine/PERF.md` describes the workload of H5 and H10 as the newest real capture without saying this. The H5 and H10 figures at evaluation 6 are therefore for the master graph, the service tree, deployment health, the report and an almost empty health model, which is one more reason not to compare them with the earlier set; the statuses do not depend on it, because the earlier set ran the whole model and is inside the targets too.
    - **H9.** Met in one run by the second hot-graph layout, 337 MB against 400 MB. Differences from the design of Ch. 11 (11.5): adjacency is a linked list per endpoint and not a CSR with a delta log, so removal is `O(degree)`; ids are strings at the API, so reads materialize elements (250 to 410 ns). Whole-engine memory at the estate size is 5.4 GB.
    - **Estate run.** One run of `sattle bench estate`: 150,000 pods, 30,000 services, 300,000 Azure resources, 240,000 active pod pairs, 1 M flow rows per 60 s window, 4 windows. Not a distribution, no p99.
    - **H4 and H12.** Measured by one `Advance` that closes windows of the master graph and commits the derived graph, not by a three-hop stack. At the estate size the close is 1.5 to 1.7 s for windows 1 to 3, above the 1 s target; the plan still keeps up with a 60 s window on one thread. The health model of Ch. 7 (7.4) is a stack of four projections on the lab capture, but its latency was not measured per hop.

## Ch. 14 Evaluation

The whole chapter as it stood before the reader rewrite (2026-11-12). The evaluation log moved to the appendix (A.9); the deviation table D1 to D37, the list of evidence files, the disagreements between documents and code, and the labels O, T, Q and D are kept only here.

    # Ch. 14 Evaluation

    **Claim.** The pipeline runs end to end on two real captures and gives one state digest per capture at five chunk sizes; three latency hypotheses are `supported` for named configurations; everything else is a proof, an example test or open.

    **Status.** The engine is that of evaluation 12, the canvas that of evaluation 11, the lab that of evaluation 6 (14.1 says what each evaluation contained). The test figures of evaluations 10 to 12 are set A's and say so. This paper replayed each real capture once in evaluation 12; its own test and canvas runs are those of evaluations 1 to 9. A figure last measured at an earlier commit says so. The evaluation is **partial**: one machine, two real captures from a cluster that no longer exists, mostly one run per figure, and ten-run figures for H5, H7, H8 and H10 taken on a machine under load (load average 14 to 115, 239 once), of which this paper repeated H5, H8 and H10 at evaluation 6 (14.7). Everything not measured is marked open, and what the prototype does not do is in one place, Ch. 15 (15.2).
    **Gate.** All: 14.0 sorts the results of this chapter by gate.
    Uses: all chapters. Evidence files are listed in 14.1. Every number below has a source file in the repository. States of the repository are cited by evaluation number; 14.1 has the table.

    ## 14.0 Results by gate

    The sections of this chapter keep their order. This table says which gate of the research plan (README, "Research plan and gates") each one serves and what its results amount to for that gate.

    | Gate | Where in this chapter | What the results amount to |
    |---|---|---|
    | 1a theory | 14.5 (determinism and batch invariance), 14.6 (theorems against tests) | Every theorem has a status and a row of test evidence. T6 and T7 hold on both real captures at five chunk sizes. The sketches and obligations of 14.6 are open |
    | 1b flexible and expressive | 14.3 (what the system holds), 14.8 (deviations in which the kernel or the language changed: D11, D22, D25, D26, D32) | A list of what was built. No result of this chapter is stated or tested as a claim of expressiveness |
    | 1c efficient | 14.7 (hypotheses), 14.9 (load on the machine) | Three hypotheses are `supported` for named configurations at the scale of the lab capture. Whole-engine memory and window close at the estate size are above target. Nothing was run at 1 million master nodes, and no budget per element exists to compare with |
    | 2a health model from real Azure data | 14.2 (the lab), 14.4 (the two captures) | The pipeline runs end to end on real data and computes a health model. Early evidence: one workload, a cluster that no longer exists |

    ## 14.1 Method and evidence

    **Evidence classes.** A claim of the paper has one of these levels. A higher level does not replace a lower one.

    | Class | Meaning | Where it comes from |
    |---|---|---|
    | A | proof in the paper (Ch. 1 to 12) | the chapter |
    | B | seeded property test: a random case generator, many cases per property | the engine's seeded tests: 8 seed groups of 500 cases, 4,000 cases per property (Artifact, A.8) |
    | C | example or conformance test on fixtures | the engine's example tests (Artifact, A.8) |
    | D | benchmark | `engine/PERF.md`, `canvas/PERF.md` |
    | E | replay of a real lab capture, or another run by this paper | `paper/evidence/*.txt`, listed per evaluation in the log below |

    **Source files used in this chapter.**
    - `engine/README.md` (what exists, deviations), `engine/PROTOCOL.md` (version 3), `engine/PERF.md` (benchmarks), `engine/tests/Sattle.Tests/` (tests), `engine/definitions/*.sattle` (fifteen definition modules).
    - `canvas/README.md`, `canvas/PERF.md` (canvas).
    - `lab/README.md`, `lab/CAPTURE-FORMAT.md` (lab, capture format, forwarder capacity).

    **Three sets of runs.** Every figure of this chapter comes from one of three sets of runs, and says which when it is not the first. *This paper* (set P): runs by the authors on a clean copy of a recorded state of the software. *Set A*: the full test and check runs that the project makes before it records a state, including the tests of a cloud database on its local emulator; the authors read their output and did not repeat them. *Set B*: runs by the developers of the engine, reported with their code and not repeated by the authors. The lab figures (time to deploy and remove the recording environment) are the lab developers' own.

    **Evaluation log.** The paper was evaluated twelve times. In evaluations 1 to 9 the authors ran the experiments themselves on a clean copy of the recorded state. From evaluation 10 on they ran no test suite: the figures are those of set A, and the authors read the code. Each row says what the software contained, what the authors ran, and what they did not. The files are in `paper/evidence/`.

    **Decision rule.** Chapter 13 (13.5) fixes the rule for hypotheses: a latency or size target is *supported* iff the statistic is at or below the target in every one of at least 10 independent runs, *refuted* iff the median run is above it, otherwise *inconclusive*. A status is stated for a configuration (store, machine, load): see 13.6 for how the rule is applied to a machine that is not the reference of Def. 13.4. A figure from fewer than ten runs is reported as *met in one run*, *not met in one run* or *not measured*. H5 (engine part, memory and SQLite), H8 (hot plane at the head revision) and H10 (restart from SQLite, with and without a state seal, at 1.2 h and 4.1 h of repeated capture) are `supported` in that sense. H7 has two labels on the Cosmos emulator from two sets of runs (`refuted` and `inconclusive`) and no status for a real account. Every other hypothesis is open (14.7).

    ## 14.2 The lab

    - **Cluster.** An AKS cluster with Azure CNI powered by Cilium (it no longer exists: its resource group was deleted on 2026-11-10, not by this project, as set A reports; the two captures were taken before that), ACNS observability enabled for Hubble relay, Kubernetes 1.34, managed Prometheus (`lab/README.md`, `lab/CAPTURE-FORMAT.md`).
    - **Workload.** Namespace `sattle-demo` with `frontend`, `api`, `db`, `loadgen` and an `intruder` that network policy denies (`lab/README.md`).
    - **Capture tooling.** A recording script records, per source, NDJSON envelopes (`v`, `source`, `capturedAt`, `seq`, `key`, `meta`, `payload`). Sources captured: Azure Resource Graph, Kubernetes list and watch, Hubble flows, Prometheus range queries, Azure Monitor metrics, Activity Log backfill, VNet flow logs and Event Hubs mirrors (`lab/CAPTURE-FORMAT.md`). A forwarder publishes the same envelopes to an Event Hub for the live input (Ch. 10, 10.6).
    - **Deployment.** The lab is an azd project rooted in `lab/` with hooks (Ch. 10, 10.5). The lab developers measured one real `azd up` (3 min 14 s) and one `azd down` (2 min 23 s) on the existing cluster, and the state after teardown against a baseline taken before (`lab/README.md`; not re-run by this paper, which cannot deploy to Azure). What teardown leaves behind: subscription deployment records, a soft-deleted storage account, ACNS leftovers in `kube-system`, and `advancedNetworking` reading `enabled: false` where it read `null`. A second `azd up` was not run.
    - **Captures used.** Two. `20261110T105738Z` (`paper/evidence/replay-head6-105738.txt`): 245,345 records (243,979 in evaluation 1, before the Event Hubs diagnostic records were read), 235,858 Hubble flows over 725 s plus 522 VNet flow log records with 175,265 tuples, a capture span of 5,679 s. `20261110T140542Z` (`replay-head6-140542.txt`): 160,300 records, 146,759 Hubble records, 1,148 VNet flow log records, records from every source, a capture span of 1,017 s. No further capture can be taken while no cluster exists. Both captures are gitignored and stay local. The tracked space file binds the health model to invented names; the real workload arguments are in a gitignored overlay next to the captures (`lab/captures/space-overlay.sattle`), which `replay` and `serve` apply to a capture under `lab/captures`. This paper's runs used a copy of that overlay that matches the committed definitions.
    - **VNet flow logs.** Tenant policy blocks public network access to the storage account. They are read through a short-lived pod in the cluster (`lab/README.md`, "Flow log blobs"). No `lost_events` records occurred in this capture.
    - **Fixtures.** `lab/fixtures/` holds scrubbed samples with the same envelope. The engine tests replay them. They were rebuilt once from the first capture with a stricter scrubber, so their state digest changed (set A reports `a90c5be7…` before and `43917169…` after; this paper cites no fixture digest elsewhere). A second fixture is the **synthetic `estate`** (`engine/tests/Sattle.Tests/fixtures/estate`, written deterministically by `build-estate.py`, invented names only: cluster `synth`, namespace `shop`, services `web`, `api` and `db`, nodes `node-a` and `node-b`; 548 records from every source the engine reads) with a space file that binds the health model to those names, so that every operator of the canvas walkthroughs has data to produce output from (Ch. 12, 12.4; `estate-head4.txt`). The tracked space file binds these invented names, so the fixture is served with `sattle serve DIR --cluster synth` and no overlay. It is also the default source of the canvas (Ch. 12, 12.4).
    - **Size.** The lab estate is small. After replay of the first capture the master graph has 15,819 entities and 5,537 relationships, of which 15,363 entities are evidence elements, kept for 24 h of logical time (Ch. 9, 9.6; without the retention rule 16,777 entities, 16,321 of them evidence elements); the other 456 entities are the estate itself. The reference estate of Ch. 11 has 750,000 entities. The lab proves the pipeline end to end, not scale.
    - **Transport.** The forwarder was measured separately: about 125 events per second per throughput unit (Ch. 10, 10.6).

    ## 14.3 The system under test

    - **Engine** (`engine/`, evaluation 12): a .NET 10 kernel with fifteen definition modules loaded from `.sattle` text (`paper/evidence/check-head9.txt` at evaluation 9, 0 errors and 0 warnings). The build treats warnings as errors (`engine/README.md`; this paper saw `sattle check` report 0 warnings and a successful build, not a compiler warning count).
    - **Adapters:** Kubernetes (with the forwarder's markers), Hubble, Azure Resource Graph, Prometheus, Activity Log (management API and Event Hubs diagnostic records), AKS audit records, Azure Monitor metrics, VNet flow logs.
    - **Language:** 19 of the 20 operator kinds (Ch. 5; `Delta` is missing), aggregates declared per context, parameters, levels written with the `lift` statement, whose mapping is a declared function or has a stated `images` policy (5.8), `lens` and `bijective` rules with backward edits (Ch. 8), identity merge with an order-independent survivor (Ch. 1), references for missing endpoints (Ch. 3).
    - **Stores:** in-memory, SQLite and Cosmos DB behind one port, with chunked commits and a writer lease on Cosmos, a journal for restart from a store, a state seal that bounds the cost of that restart, a served history capped at 2,000 patches per graph and 20,000 events, and batched Cosmos writes (Ch. 10, 11; Cosmos against the emulator only).
    - **Protocol version 3:** drafts, releases, preview, refactoring, value roles (on deployment health and on the health model), document projections, content tags, lineage in both directions, references alone, meter declarations and references in the static output (Ch. 2, 4, 5, 7, 9, 12).
    - **Live input:** an Event Hubs reader that resumes from the journal (Ch. 10; not run here, its loop is tested with a fake source).
    - **Examples:** the health model of Ch. 7, deployment health and its report (Ch. 2, 12), the service and capability levels (Ch. 5).
    - **Canvas** (`canvas/`, the canvas of evaluation 11, Ch. 12; this paper's runs are at the canvas of evaluation 8): data, space, ontology and document views, the projection editor, default coloring from value roles, the default source from engine output, the abstraction highlight, reference stubs and collapsed supporting elements, and a `Lift` card.
    - **Lab** (`lab/`, the lab of evaluation 6): the azd project of Ch. 10.
    - **Tests.** Engine at evaluation 12, from set A: `Sattle.Tests` 670 of 670 in every category, and `Sattle.Differential` 149 of 149 against the independent reference evaluator. At evaluation 10: 639 of 639 in the full run with the Cosmos emulator (17 min 45 s under load). Engine at evaluation 9, this paper (`tests-head9.txt`): 0 failures of 463 tests in the default run (2 m 7 s here), of 96 in the slow category and of 20 in the capture category, 579 in all, without the Cosmos cases (set A reports 598 of 598 with the emulator at that commit, 19 more). Canvas (`canvas-head9.txt`): 643 tests in 33 files, 0 failures, against no engine; the canvas scripts ran against the engine at evaluation 7 (evaluation 8) and at evaluation 9 (evaluation 9).

    What is not built is in the limits table of Ch. 15 (15.2).

    ## 14.4 End-to-end result on the real captures

    From `paper/evidence/replay-head16-105738.txt`, `replay-head16-140542.txt` and `health-head16.txt`: one replay of each capture by this paper with the Release build of the engine of evaluation 12, default options, the local space overlay applied. Wall times are single runs on a shared laptop and not performance figures. The digests and counts differ from those of evaluation 7 to evaluation 10 (`sha256:4df6bedf…8b775` and `sha256:2a0b72c2…be93e`, `replay-head7-*.txt`, `replay-head9-*.txt`) because the engine changed in four ways at evaluation 12: one activity event has one identity, so `ActivityEvent` elements and their `Concerns` edges are fewer (Ch. 10, 10.6); meters are elements of the master; telemetry rows are counted as support, which raises the sample counts; and the fixes of the defects found by the differential harness. The definitions now also hold the five graphs of the paused estate health tree (`treeSignals`, `treeStructure`, `treeRollup`, `healthTree`, `healthReport`); they are in the replay output and are not evidence of anything here.

    In evaluation 1, before evidence elements and Deployments were read, the master of the first capture had 434 entities and 408 relationships at revision 168 (`replay-latest.txt`). The growth since is the evidence elements (Ch. 9, 9.6) and the 22 Deployments. The master telemetry sample count is the same, 4,595.

    Independent check recorded in `engine/PERF.md`, for an earlier slice: the `flows` and `drops` telemetry summed in the master graph (199,379 and 512) equals an independent count of the capture with `jq`, and 199,379 of the 235,858 flows are between two named pods. It was not repeated for the evidence elements: the counts of Ch. 10, 10.6 are the engine's own and no independent count of `ActivityEvent` or `AuditEvent` elements was made.

    **What this shows.** The pipeline of Ch. 10 and 5 runs end to end on real data from every source of the lab: adapters produce rows, the ingestion projection resolves identity and emits facts and evidence elements, flow counts are telemetry on `Calls` relationships (rule A1), derived graphs roll them up with a hysteresis status, a stack of four projections computes a health model that finds a failing service on the second capture, a separate ontology computes deployment health with presentation roles, and none of it creates a revision per sample (674 master revisions for 235,858 flows and 175,265 flow tuples).
    **What it does not show.** Scale, the Cosmos store on a real capture or a real account, `sync` rules, live input (built, not run here), a stack of lens links, a capability level on real data, and that the unhealthy states match the real condition of the cluster.

    ## 14.5 Determinism and batch invariance

    **T7 (replay determinism, Theorem 6.3).**
    - A test replays the fixtures twice: two replays of the fixtures give the same state digest and, for every graph, pairwise equal patches and equal delta encodings. Class C.
    - Two tests run on the real recordings: every capture present is replayed at chunk sizes 1, 50 and 100,000 with the replay defaults and the state digest, the number of identity conflicts (0) and the digest of every graph are compared. Class E. This paper ran the capture category with both captures linked into the export in evaluations 4 to 7 and 9, and all tests passed (18 tests, 20 from evaluation 7 on; `tests-head3.txt`, `tests-head4.txt`, `tests-head6.txt`, `tests-head7.txt`, `tests-head9.txt`). This paper's own replays at five sizes are below.

    **T6 (batch invariance, Theorem 6.2).**
    - A test with chunk sizes 1, 2, 3, 7, 50 and 100,000 against 2000: equal state digests, and the integrated output equals the final graph (the Quine invariant of O6.1). Class C on fixtures.
    - **B1 and B2 have targeted tests:** one resource under two spellings of its id counts into one window under every batching (B1, three cases); a row for a window beyond the late horizon is dropped, counted and replaces nothing (B2, four cases); a row inside the horizon revises its window (the other side of B2). They pass in the default run. Each test fails when its fix is reverted (`mutation-head6.txt`, this paper's repeat of set B's check): with the groups of `acme.ops` keyed by the raw `resourceId`, the three B1 cases fail; with the drop of a row beyond the late horizon disabled in the engine, the four B2 cases fail and the two cases inside the horizon still pass. Class C. The suite had no such tests in evaluations 4 and 5 (found by searching `engine/tests/`); in that time only the capture tests guarded the two premises.
    - **D17 resolved.** In evaluation 1 chunk size 50 gave a different digest on the first capture, with one differing Activity Log window (3 against 2). Two causes, both fixed (Ch. 6, 6.7). Since evaluation 2 (`replay-chunks-head.txt`) the default chunking (1,636 chunks), 50, 777 and 100,000 give the **same state digest** on `20261110T105738Z`, and the 4,595 master telemetry samples are the same.
    - **D21 resolved.** In evaluation 2 chunk size 1 gave a different digest on the first capture (eight `Calls` relationships with a different endpoint id, identity counters 0 conflicts against 6; `chunk-1-defect.txt`). Merging two identities that denote one entity made the digests equal at five sizes, but the number of merges still depended on the chunk size (1 at 50 and above, 0 at 1; `replay-chunks-merge.txt`). With the survivor chosen as the smallest id minted for any key of the entity (Ch. 1, Proposition 1.8) the result is **one digest per capture at all five sizes** (default, 1, 50, 777, 100,000) with the same number of merges at every size (35 and 43) and 0 conflicts, in evaluations 4 to 7 and 9 (`replay-chunks-head3.txt`, `replay-chunks-head4.txt`, `replay-chunks-head6.txt`, `replay-chunks-head7.txt`, `replay-chunks-head9.txt`). At evaluation 7 to evaluation 10 the digests are `sha256:4df6bedf…8b775` on `20261110T105738Z` and `sha256:2a0b72c2…be93e` on `20261110T140542Z`. At evaluation 12 they are `sha256:cad8907e…89d4` and `sha256:6de23264…2370`: this paper replayed each capture once at the default chunking, and set B reports the same digest at chunk sizes 1, 50 and 100,000 with 0 conflicts, which neither set A nor this paper repeated. Chunking changes how many provisional identities are minted before the merge (16,722 at chunk size 1 against 17,564 at 100,000 on the first capture, evaluation 4), and the merge makes the result the same. The document of the report has one digest at chunk sizes 1 and 100,000 although the graph has 60 and 39 revisions (`document-head3.txt`, `document-head4.txt`, `document-head6.txt`).
    - **Reading.** Theorem 6.2 holds on the graphs, telemetry and documents of both captures under five chunk sizes. Its premises B1 and B2 are covered by targeted tests and by the capture digests. B3 (identity independent of arrival order) holds on these two inputs, on the merge examples of the engine's tests and on one synthetic input in two arrival orders at four chunk sizes; Proposition 1.8 states it with a sketch. **No generator produces random key arrival orders**, so B3 is a tested premise and not a proved or generally tested one.

    **What this does not cover.** Different interleavings of independent sources beyond the shuffled arrival of the live reproducibility tests (distance up to 40 places, three seeds, on the fixtures; one more on the newest capture). Repeating the same replay many times on a real capture. Different platforms (Q13). Determinism of a **Cosmos** run on a real capture: sets B and A report an equal digest on the emulator before and after the batched writes (Ch. 11, 11.6), which this paper did not repeat. A live run replayed from its recorded stream on the real Event Hubs reader (Proposition 10.7 is tested against a stand-in). The SQLite store was tested for seal digests and the replays ran in memory. The state digest of a repeated capture (the history of H10) is the same whether the run took seals or not (`sha256:833e7e6d…ec601` and `sha256:7aa3ac42…dcbc6`, set A without seals, this paper with a seal every 2,000 entries).

    ## 14.6 Theorems against tests

    Every numbered statement of the paper has a status: proved, proved relative to a cited theorem, proved under a named premise, or tested only ([inventory.md](inventory.md)). Tests can support a proof, never replace it. They are of three kinds:

    - **Seeded random tests.** A generator produces random cases from fixed seeds, 4,000 cases per property, so a run can be repeated exactly. They cover the algebra of changes and the versions of a graph (Ch. 3, 4), with one caveat: the generator keeps only changes that the prototype itself accepts, so a wrong rejection cannot be seen.
    - **Comparison with an independent implementation.** A second, simple implementation computes every result from scratch, from the definitions of this paper. The prototype's step-by-step result is compared with it on generated inputs, under every way of cutting the input into batches and with reordering inside a batch. This tests Theorems 6.1 and 6.2 per operator kind; `Lift` and `Fixpoint` are not covered independently, because the simple implementation uses the prototype's own translation of them.
    - **Example tests** on small inputs and on the two recordings.

    Which test exercises which statement is listed in the appendix (Artifact, A.8).

    ## 14.7 Performance results against the hypotheses of Ch. 13

    Source for the figures: `engine/PERF.md` (set B's, not re-run by this paper except where stated), set A's ten-run output for H5, H7, H8 and H10 (`paper/evidence/perf-*-orch*.txt`, copied unchanged) and this paper's own ten runs of the same harness on clean exports of evaluation 4 (`perf-*-paper.txt`), evaluation 5 (`perf-*-head4-paper.txt`) and evaluation 6 (`perf-*-head6-paper.txt`). The rows for H1 to H4, H6 and H9 are the first evaluation's figures, unchanged; the rows for H5, H7, H8 and H10 are ten-run figures, **taken at a load average of 14 to 115 (239 for one set of three restarts) while other contributors ran, with no quiet machine available**, so latencies are pessimistic and the spread wide. Machine: Apple M1 Max, 10 cores, 64 GB, macOS, .NET 10.0.14, Release build, one writer thread, one run per figure unless a range is given. **This is not the reference hardware of Def. 13.4** (an Azure VM SKU). The labels follow the rule of 13.6 with the scope clause stated there: a status belongs to a configuration. At evaluation 6 the benchmarks of H5 and H10 run the real capture with the tracked space file, in which the health model is bound to invented names, so the health topology and state are empty in them (`bench-overlay-head6.txt`, Ch. 13, 13.7).

With telemetry on all 287,968 edges and 99,485 nodes (1,062,871 values per tick) the page kept up with 60 ticks per second, about 64 million values per second, at 53.4 to 60 fps while panning. After the move to the React shell: pan 60.0 fps, telemetry at 60 ticks per second 59.8 fps (achieved 61.4 ticks per second), JS heap 308 to 470 MB. A first run with the shell reached only 33 ticks per second; the cause was a store action per frame, fixed by coalescing (`canvas/PERF.md`). Not measured: Safari, Firefox, a stream arriving while panning, a real engine transport at scale, the editor above tens of cards. These are viewer figures on synthetic data and do not bear on the engine hypotheses.

    ## 14.8 Deviations found by the prototype

    A deviation is a point where the prototype differs from the design of the earlier chapters or of the research documents. Each row states the difference as it is now; a row marked resolved also states what it was. The chapter named discusses it. The engine README keeps the same list.

    | Id | Deviation | Chapter | Effect on the paper |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | D1 | instants are nanoseconds in a signed 64-bit integer (years 1678 to 2262), decimals are 28-digit .NET `decimal`, not unbounded | 1 | N1 to N3 allow arbitrary precision. A bounded implementation needs a stated range and a typed error beyond it |
    | D2 | module syntax has an optional version `module m @ 1.0.0;` | 2, 5 | the grammar had no place for a release version |
    | D3 | `Correspondence.source` and `.target` are `sourceRefs` and `targetRefs`; canonicalizers are separate nodes (`canon.LowerInvariant`) | 2 | one property name keeps one kind per module (K-rule on properties) |
    | D4 | no `Key`, `Identity` or `Evidence` graph nodes. Bindings are an append-only table written with the master commit | 1, 2 | Def. 2.12 and the identity catalog as typed graph data are not realized. The model of Def. 1.10 is realized as a table |
    | D5 (resolved) | a `Link` whose right key is bound to another identity of the same type merges the two identities with a forwarding record. The survivor is the smallest id minted for any key of the entity, including keys that nothing had used yet (an earlier rule depended on arrival order, D21). Different types are a conflict and ignored | 1, 5 | Theorem 1.7 and Proposition 1.8 tested by examples; concurrent projections and generated arrival orders untested (O1.2) |
    | D6 | relationship ids hash `[tenant, output ontology module, key rule, source id, target id]`. The paper earlier required the graph id | 1, 5 | **resolved by changing the paper** (Ch. 5, Def. 5.9), because refactoring preservation needs ids independent of the graph. Confirmed by the project |
    | D7 | closed-window records are not recorded as separate inputs. Windows are closed inside the projection by `Group` over `Window` and by explicit `Advance`, and replay **recomputes** them from the raw rows | 7, 6 | Theorem 7.3 is argued from recorded closed records (Def. 7.7). The engine's replay is deterministic only while the raw rows are the recorded input, which holds in replay. Under the live ring with overflow the premise changes, so Def. 7.7 is needed before live input |
    | D8 | the telemetry port gains `MarkPartial` and a `Deleted` flag on samples (window retraction) | 7, 11 | Def. 11.4 lacks retraction. Needed for `revise` (Prop. 6.8) |
    | D9 | closed windows stay revisable for 3 window widths (`LateHorizonWindows`), then their state is dropped, and a later row for them is dropped and counted. `replay` defaults to a horizon of 1,000,000 and has `--late-horizon WINDOWS`; `serve` and the live input keep 3 | 7 | premise B2 of Theorem 6.2. Def. 7.6 has no horizon. A live run and its replay need the same horizon (Prop. 10.7, tested with `--late-horizon 3` against a stand-in for the reader) |
    | D10 | the storage port is synchronous, `Follow` returns a list. The Cosmos store materializes inside `Commit`, so there is no watermark lag and no overlay | 11 | the contract's streams are replaced inside the process, as Ch. 11 suggests; H13 does not apply to any adapter |

No quantified paths in `Match` | 5, 6 | T9 and T10 have acyclic example evidence only. The typing rules have negative example tests. Reasons for `Delta` and the `Union` policies: nothing to test them on |

    | D13 | `v.p` read columns are not carried past `Group`, `Rollup` or `Threshold` | 5 | consistent with the typing rules (Group outputs keys and aggregates only) |

Ch. 13 names BenchmarkDotNet for micro results |

    | D16 | canvas and engine mismatches of the first export | 12 | resolved or open as listed in 12.5; open: time alignment by `rev_of`, type ids by name |

Two causes: one resource id in two spellings grouped as two keys, and late rows reviving dropped windows. Both fixed | 6, 7 | Theorem 6.2 gains the premises B1 and B2. Re-run: digests equal at 50, 777, 100,000 and the default |

Merging the identities gave one digest at five sizes with a merge count that still depended on the size; the order-independent survivor (D5) gives one digest and one merge count (35 and 43) at five sizes on both captures | 1, 6 | premise B3 of Theorem 6.2 holds on these inputs and on one synthetic two-order input; no generator of arrival orders |

    | D23 | the Cosmos store differs from the commit protocol of 11.6: materialization inside the commit call, identity bindings after the commit record, the watermark as an upsert in `leases`, a group of more than 100 records as several batches, `identity` and `leases` partitioned `/t, /g, …` and not as 11.5, the `lens` container not created, and a lease with an owner and an expiry and no epoch. Built: chunked commits (chunks first, commit record last), lease enforcement, materialization as one transactional batch per touched logical partition, identity bindings as one batch per domain partition, no read before a write, the watermark every 64 commits. Run only against the vNext emulator, by sets B and A, plus replays of a real capture with a digest equal to memory: 230 s and 67,181 requests (set A), 276 s and 67,193 (set B); before the batched writes 1,607 s and 195,669 requests | 11 | Theorems 11.2 and 11.3 are exercised for Cosmos by the conformance cases on the emulator; the overlay and watermark steps are not; no real account measured; the emulator has no RU figure |
    | D24 | documents are graphs of `Block` entities of the ontology `sattle.doc` rendered by one generic renderer, not a document model with an order list independent of the blocks; no completeness flag or `ExplicitlyTruncated`. No kernel sink | 12 | Def. 12.5 and Theorem 12.7 hold on the model, and the engine realizes graph patches (Theorem 4.3); truncation is open (O12.7) |
    | D25 | the kernel gained the operator kind `Retain` (the nineteenth). Evidence elements are retained by `retain on time for 24h` in the three evidence rules of `acme.ops`; the age is a duration with a unit in `/palette` | 2, 5, 9 | Def. 2.2 and 5.3 amended, Def. 5.15 and Proposition 5.9 added; the kernel digest moved; the 24 h is set B's choice |
    | D26 | aggregates are declared per context in `/palette` (`Group` over data, `Rollup` over telemetry); `weightedMean` and `last` are `Group` only, `mean` over a `Rollup` needs a gauge | 5, 12 | the engine's rule is stricter than Def. 5.6, and it settles where the aggregates added by D11 may be used over telemetry; O5.7 |
    | D27 | a served space keeps a journal in its store (SQLite table, Cosmos items) and restarts by replaying it with verification of every commit; without a seal the cost is linear in the journal; drafts, the telemetry ring beyond what replay recomputes and subscriptions are not stored | 9, 11 | Ch. 9 had the input log as a source of truth and Ch. 11 a seal plus a log tail; the engine has the first, and a seal of another kind (D28) (Def. 11.10, Proposition 11.9, H10) |
    | D28 | the engine's seal is a **state seal**: a compressed field-by-field snapshot of the whole state of one engine build after the first `n` journal entries, stored next to the journal (a SQLite table, chunk items in Cosmos), taken every `--seal-every` entries (default 5000) while the host holds its lock, valid only for the build that wrote it, and not the page set of Def. 11.8 in object storage. A restart loads it, checks the build, kernel, catalog and state digests and replays the entries after it | 11 | Def. 11.11 and Proposition 11.10 added. A restart from a seal does not recompute the journal before it (found by this paper, `restart-seal-prefix-head4.txt`). This is the documented trust model of a seal: the start message says what came from the seal, and `serve --verify-journal` replays the whole journal and checks the seal's digest (`restart-seal-prefix-head6.txt`); the first restart after a deploy takes the no-seal path; the page-set seal of Def. 11.8 is still not built |
    | D29 | every ETag is a digest of the content it names, and only resources addressed by a content digest are `immutable`; revision-addressed resources (`snapshot?revision=`, `patches?after=&to=`, `document?revision=`) are `no-cache` and revalidated. The engine first used a graph name and a revision (`"snap-master-17"`, `"onto-health-16"`) and marked revision-addressed responses `immutable` | 12 | **found in set A**: a revision or catalog revision is unique only within one run of one store, so after a rebuild with changed definitions a browser held `"onto-health-16"`, was told `304` and kept an ontology without value roles. Proposition 12.10 is restated (a) within a run and (b) across runs; protocol 3.9 |
    | D30 | the host serves from a bounded history: the last 2,000 patches per graph and the last 20,000 events by default (`--retain-patches`, `--retain-events`); older requests answer `410 Gap` or `gap`; the catalog history is not capped | 11, 12 | the contract and Ch. 12 had the whole history (Def. 12.7 had a ring of 100,000 events); heads, state and digests do not depend on the cap; it is what keeps a state seal the same size at any history length (Ch. 13, H10) |
    | D33 (resolved) | the engine evaluated `Threshold` as a function of the windows in the look-back of its `Series`, so a key that entered `high` and stayed inside the band went back to `low` when the windows in which it entered aged out (found by the code review and the reference evaluator). Fixed at evaluation 12: episodes with a carried state | 6, 7 | Def. 6.7 and 7.10 state the semantics; the comparison with the independent implementation and a targeted test (a state inside the band survives the look-back) test it. The health states on both captures did not change |
    | D34 (resolved except for the report) | conflicting fragments: the engine let the last sink win and, inside one sink, the last arrival. Since evaluation 12 it applies the rule of Def. 6.4 (a function of the fragment multiset, a conflicting property absent, the smallest `rid` kept, negative support kept) and counts conflicts per run. A commit does not yet list its conflicts | 6 | Def. 6.4 restated. 0 conflicts of every kind on both captures (`replay-head16-*.txt`) |
    | D36 | on an identity merge the engine adds the telemetry windows of the two ids for every property | 7 | Law 7.5, item 3, restated: combine by the property's rule for `sum`, `min`, `max`, a conflict otherwise. Right for `sum`, not built for the rest |
    | D37 | on an identity merge a `Group` that is keyed by the identity is not re-keyed: the two groups stay. Their data fragments meet at the sink (equal values are one value, different values are a property conflict, Def. 6.4), and telemetry rows that arrive after the link through the two groups meet under the rule of premise B1 and not under the combine rule of the property. Reported by set B; not built otherwise | 6, 7 | Law 7.5, item 3, and Def. 6.4 say what the result must be; the engine reaches it only where the two groups agree |
    | D35 | the in-memory telemetry store drops windows 48 h behind the newest window start it has seen, silently | 7, 11 | Def. 7.13 added: retention is a function of the logical frontier and of declared data, checked against `within`, and a read behind it says so. Not implemented |
    | D31 | serving a recorded capture used the live late horizon of three windows, so rows with old timestamps were dropped as late: on the newest capture 192,095 rows, and the health model showed 9 `Healthy` and 2 `Unknown` instead of 7 `Healthy` and 4 `Unhealthy` (set B). Fixed: `serve CAPTURE` uses the replay horizon, stored with the journal and the seals; only `serve --live` keeps 3 | 7, 10 | a served capture is a replay. The walkthrough previews of `health` on a served newest capture (347 entities and 353 relationships, evaluation 6, Ch. 12) were taken under the short horizon and are superseded by the replay figures. The live case with backfilled input stays a limit |
    | D32 (resolved) | a level was written as about twenty ordinary statements (join with the image, drop self-loops, key by the pair of images, roll up, recompute the ratio, derive the state again), with nothing that required the image to be a function. Now one `lift` statement: the kernel gained the operator kind `Lift` (the twentieth; 54 concrete types with `LiftState`), the planner lowers it to those operators, and the checker requires a mapping declared a function or an `images` policy. Cardinality is not checked at ingest | 2, 5 | Def. 5.16 to 5.18 and Propositions 5.10 to 5.12 unchanged; 5.8 describes the statement and its lowering; O5.8 discharged except for ingest and generated tests |

    ## 14.9 Threats to validity

    **Construct.**
    - The hypotheses of Ch. 13 name Azure hardware and Cosmos. The measurements are on a laptop-class machine with in-memory and SQLite stores and, for the Cosmos store, the vNext emulator, which reports a request charge of 1 and is not a latency model of a real account. A pass here is not a pass there, and `H3`, `H7`, `H13` cannot be confirmed without a real Cosmos account.
    - H4 and H12 were measured with one `Advance` call that includes two graphs. The hypothesis concerns a per-hop latency over a stack of at least three projections.
    - The synthetic benchmark workload has 5,000 pair series (`engine/PERF.md`). The reference estate has about 240,000 flow pairs.

    **Internal.**
    - Mostly one run per figure, on a machine that was shared with other work sessions in the case of the canvas. `engine/PERF.md` reports no variance for the single-run figures; the ten-run figures (H5, H7, H8, H10) report median, minimum, maximum and an order-statistic interval. **A machine with no other load was not available to set A, set B or this paper**: set A's ten-run figures were taken at a load average of 25 to 106 at evaluation 4 and 25 to 75 at evaluation 5 (recorded per run in `perf-*-orch*.txt`), set B's at 14 to 115, and the runs of this paper at 27 to 91 at evaluation 4, 16 to 70 at evaluation 5 (one set of three restarts at 100 to 239) and 16 to 82 at evaluation 6. The label a figure gets is therefore scoped to that load (Ch. 13, 13.5). For an upper-bound target, load can only raise latencies, so a pass under load holds a fortiori and a failure under load is weak evidence; the one failure, H7 on the emulator, is also on a store that is not the target. The estate run is one run of four windows.
    - The property tests are by the same authors as the proofs. The generators are seeded and the properties test the engine types, not an independent implementation. Only the kernel has mutation tests.
    - Several theorem statements were checked on fixtures and on one real capture, not on randomly generated projections (T5, 6.1; 5.2).
    - Wall times of replays and test runs are single runs on a laptop that other contributors were using, so they are not performance figures (default engine run of 456 tests 1 m 52 s at evaluation 7; 403 tests in 2 m 6 s, 96 slow tests in 5 m 4 s and 18 capture tests in 3 m 52 s at evaluation 6, at load 45 to 90). Evaluation 1 replayed with a Debug build, every later one with a Release build. The capture-category tests need the two real captures linked into the export; in evaluations 1 to 3 they were not, and the tests that use a capture returned early.
    - The investigation of D21 compared exported snapshots and telemetry with `jq` and did not instrument the engine, so its first cause was a reading of counters. That the merge removes the mismatch is a measurement on two inputs, and that the order-independent survivor removes the dependence in general is a sketch (Proposition 1.8) with one synthetic test and two captures. The causes of D17 were found by set B, not by this paper. The unit tests of B1 and B2 were absent from the suite in evaluations 4 and 5; a regression of the D17 causes was caught only by the capture tests in that time, and only on the shapes in those captures.
    - From evaluation 10 on the paper's numbers at the current commit are set A's and not this paper's: the people who direct the work also verify it, and this paper's check is a reading of code and tests. The evidence files in `paper/evidence/` end at evaluation 9.
    - Canvas and engine were evaluated at different times. The canvas tests, the walkthroughs on the `estate` and the accessibility script are at the canvas of evaluation 8 against the engines of evaluation 7 and evaluation 9. The canvas at the canvas of evaluation 11, with its `Lift` card, its regenerated default source and the references route, was checked in set A and not by this paper. The walkthrough row on the newest real capture is in evaluation 6 and was not repeated. What the canvas shows on the served newest capture (589 stubs, 143 collapsed evidence elements, the propagated failure) was seen in a browser in set A and is not asserted by a script.
    - Every evaluation is of a commit, not of the working tree, which held uncommitted changes of other contributors. The real-capture runs depend on a gitignored overlay file that only this machine has and that other contributors edit; this paper used a copy that matches the committed definitions (the state digests confirm the copy), after one batch of runs in evaluation 6 failed to load with the shared file and was repeated.
    - The lab figures (azd times, state after teardown) are the lab developers' own measurements. This paper did not deploy to Azure and cannot repeat them, and nobody can at present: the cluster no longer exists.
    - **Disagreements between the repository's documents and its code** as last checked by this paper (engine documents at evaluation 9, canvas README at the canvas of evaluation 11). (1) `engine/PERF.md` gives 578 tests for the full run without the emulator; this paper counts 579 as the sum of its three runs (463, 96 and 20), which agrees with set A's 598 with the emulator less the 19 Cosmos cases. The default count, 463, agrees. (2) A comment in the viewer's source says that telemetry tick k is aligned with revision k (read at the canvas of evaluation 8, not rechecked). The contradiction in `canvas/README.md` about relationships with a missing endpoint is gone at the canvas of evaluation 11. Gone since the last evaluation: `engine/PERF.md` now says that the benchmarks load the tracked space without the local overlay (so the health chain is almost empty in H5 and H10, `bench-overlay-head6.txt`) and gives current test counts. The paper uses its own counts and says so.

    **External.**
    - Two captures from one cluster with a demo workload and a load generator. They are not a representative estate (a few hundred non-evidence entities). They have no `lost_events` and no forwarder markers in their direct sources, no pod name reuse (Q5 not evaluated) and no late-arriving Hubble history beyond what the replay handled. The VNet flow logs are 2 h of one cluster.
    - The real captures are git-ignored and stay local, the cluster they came from no longer exists, and the space binding they need is a gitignored overlay. A third party can reproduce the replays of the fixtures and of the synthetic `estate` (both in the repository, with invented or scrubbed names) and the synthetic benchmarks, but not the real-capture figures, the ten-run H5 and H10 figures (their workload is the newest capture) or anything that needs Azure. Those rest on `paper/evidence/*.txt` and on the claim that the captures exist.
    - No comparison with Feldera, Materialize, Memgraph or Neo4j (B15, B16), so the paper makes no claim of relative performance.

    **Conclusion validity.**
    - Statuses use the rule of Ch. 13 as written, with the scope clause of 13.6. Where a figure is far from its target (H9) or far inside it (H6, H2), a single run is still a single run. The three `supported` statuses (H5 engine part, H8, H10) are inside their targets (a factor of about 77 for H5 on SQLite for the records that made a revision, about 2,000 for all records in memory, about 2 for H8 on its worst run of any set, and about 5.7 for H10 with a seal on its worst run and 1.5 without one, under load), and each is for one configuration on a machine under load; none says anything about the Cosmos store or the reference hardware. H8 is the closest to its target. H7 has two different labels on the emulator from two sets, which says that the emulator figure is not a property of the design.
    - Evidence that the engine matches the *paper* is limited to the listed tests. Where no test exists (T9, T10, T12, T13, provenance, a stack of lens links), the paper's claims stand as proofs only. Several statements (Proposition 1.8 on the survivor, Proposition 5.7 on refactoring, Proposition 5.9 on retention, Propositions 7.7 and 7.8 on the health model, Proposition 9.11 on evidence elements, Proposition 10.7 on live runs, Proposition 10.8 on live resume, Proposition 11.9 on restart, Proposition 11.10 on restart from a state seal, Proposition 12.10 on tags, the premises B1 to B3 of Theorem 6.2) are proof sketches with example tests or none. The health states on the second capture show the roll-up working and do not show that the cluster was unhealthy.
